#### P013 | 10.1021/acsami.6b09989 | Optimization of Pore Structure of Cathodic Carbon Supports for Solvate Ionic Liquid Electrolytes Based Lithium-Sulfur Batteries

· Optimization of Pore Structure of Cathodic Carbon Supports for Solvate Ionic Liquid Electrolytes Based Lithium-Sulfur Batteries Shiguo Zhang,† Ai Ikoma,† Zhe Li, Kazuhide Ueno, Xiaofeng Ma, Kaoru Dokko, and Masayoshi Watanabe* Department of Chemistry and Biotechnology, Yokohama National University, 79-5 Tokiwadai, Hodo…
· However, their practical application remains a challenge because of the serve shuttle eﬀect caused by the dissolution of polysulfides in common organic electrolytes.
· Polysulfide- insoluble electrolytes, such as solvate ionic liquids (ILs), have recently emerged as alternative candidates and shown great potential in suppressing the shuttle eﬀect and improving the cycle stability of Li-S batteries.
· Redox electrochemical reactions in polysulfide-insoluble electrolytes occur via a solid-state process at the interphase between the electrolyte and the composite cathode; therefore, creating an appropriate interface between sulfur and a carbon support is of great importance.
· Nevertheless, the porous carbon supports established for conventional organic electrolytes may not be suitable for polysulfide-insoluble electrolytes.
· In this work, we investigated the eﬀect of the porous structure of carbon materials on the Li-S battery performance in polysulfide-insoluble electrolytes using solvate ILs as a model electrolyte.
· The battery performance in both polysulfide-soluble electrolytes and solvate ILs was used to study the eﬀect of electrolytes.
· We propose a plausible mechanism to explain the diﬀerent porous structure requirements in polysulfide-soluble and polysulfide-insoluble electrolytes.
· KEYWORDS: porous carbon, lithium-sulfur battery, electrolyte, solvate ionic liquids, polysulfide-insoluble, pore volume 1.
· In addition, the active materials undergo severe volumetric expansion/contraction between sulfur and Li2S during repeated cycles, which results in decreased mechanical integrity and poor electrode stability.11,12 Moreover, the lithium polysulfide intermediates (Li2Sm, especially 4 ≤m ≤8) tend to dissolve in most organi…
· This provides a high ability to dissociate alkali metal salts and thus a high solubility of the polysulfide species, especially for high-order lithium polysulfides (Li2Sm, 4 ≤m ≤8).
· For example, S solubility (as Li2Sm) can exceed 10 M in tetrahydrofuran.27 The solubility of one of the most soluble polysulfides with the nominal formula Li2S8 can be up to ≈6 M (total atomic S concentration) in a 1 M LiTFSA/G4 electrolyte (G4: tetraethylene glycol dimethyl ether or so-called tetraglyme).28 Therefore,…
· In this case, redox reactions of S species take place through a solid-liquid process between the electronic conductor (C/S composite) and Li+ conductor (electrolyte containing polysulfides).
· Several new electrolytes (e.g., ionic liquids (ILs)23,35-37 and concentrated solutions25,38), which are distinct from the catholyte-type electrolytes, showed great potential in suppress- ing the shuttle eﬀect and improving the cycle stability.
· In particular, certain glymeLi salt molten complexes (e.g., [Li(G4)][TFSA], Figure 2), as a new family of solvate ILs,39 have proven to be promising electrolytes for Li-S bat- teries.19,33,40 The solvate ILs can be obtained by simply mixing equimolar amounts of glymes and Li salts, whereas conven- tional IL electrolyte…
· Solvate ILs are intrinsically Li+ conductive, have a high Li+ concentration, and a high Li+ transference number.19,32,33,40-45 More importantly, all of the oxygen atoms of the ether molecules in the solvate ILs coordinate with the doped Li salts.
· In this respect, the porous carbons specific to conventional ether electrolytes may not be suitable for solvate ILs.
· Based on an investigation of the Li-S battery performance of a series of porous carbons containing micropores, mesopores, or macropores in solvate ILs, this work aims to optimize the pore structure of cathodic carbon supports for Li-S cells with electrolytes in which polysulfides are insoluble. 2.

· The polysulfides (initially formed at the cathode) can dissolve into the electrolyte and diﬀuse to the anode side (especially for high-order polysulfides), where they react with metal lithium to form low-order polysulfides and diﬀuse back to the cathode side.
· Since there are indeed numerous studies of the reversible charge/discharge of micro-/mesoporous composite cathodes in LiNO3-free poly- sulfide-soluble electrolyte systems,9,56,62-68 the deterioration of IOC50(PFA)/S in LiTFSA-DOL/DME could be attributed to the pore structure of the cathodic supports.
· Carbons with a large pore size and large pore volume are more suitable for Li- S batteries using solvate IL electrolytes, rather than the widely accepted micro-/mesoporous carbons that work well for conventional polysulfide-soluble electrolyte systems.
· The following models, shown in Figure 10, could explain the distinct requirements for pore structure between polysulfide- insoluble and polysulfide-soluble electrolytes.
· As for conven- tional polysulfide-soluble electrolytes, the initial active material (S8) was first transformed into Li2Sm upon lithiation and dissolved into the electrolytes, forming a catholyte.
· In general, a high surface area is expected to be eﬀective for adsorbing Li2Sm and preventing it from diﬀusing into the bulk electrolyte.
· The micropores help to confine the polysulfides, while mesopores enable better electrolyte access and better ion transport.16,69 In contrast, macroporous carbons, such as IOCs, do not seem to be suitable for polysulfide-soluble electrolyte systems because the active material tends to form a relatively thin layer on the…
· Both S8 and Li2Sm can easily dissolve in ether electrolytes, which results in severe shuttle eﬀects and dramatic loss of the active materials from the cathode, as shown in Figure 10a.
· The in situ formed polysulfides in polysulfide-insoluble electrolytes (such as solvate ILs) cannot dissolve and diﬀuse into the electrolyte during the discharge process.
· The porous structure will also help the electrolytes to wet the cathode and provide ionic transport.
· In this respect, highly porous IOC(PFA)s would be a favorable cathodic host for polysulfide-insoluble electrolytes.

· CONCLUSIONS In conclusion, a series of porous carbons containing ordered or disordered macropores, micropores, and mesopores were prepared and used as cathodic supports for Li-S batteries exploring solvate IL based electrolytes.
· Therefore, carbons with both a large pore size and large volume, rather than the widely accepted mesopores and/or micropores that work well for conventional polysulfide-soluble electrolytes, are more suitable for solvate ILs.
· However, IOC50(PFA) does not work well in the LiTFSA-DOL/DME system, suggesting that carbons with macropores may not be suitable for polysulfide-soluble electrolyte systems due to a severe shuttle eﬀect.

· The use of fumed silica as an electrolyte additive therefore significantly improves the specific charge and Coulombic eﬃciency of lithium-sulfur batteries.
· As PSs are soluble in the electrolyte, they can diﬀuse within it and be reduced at the negative electrode (metallic lithium), resulting in a loss of active material and the formation of an insulating layer, and ultimately in an increase of the cell resistance.
· As a result, the Coulombic eﬃciency of Li-S batteries rarely exceeds 80%, and only with the help of electrolyte additives, such as LiNO3, can it be increased to a level close to 100% (refs 17, 18).

· Fumed SiO2 as an electrolyte additive To further explore the practical significance of the adsorption of long-chain polysulfides on the SiO2, we tested fumed SiO2 as an electrolyte additive in the standard coin-type cells.
· We found that the presence of SiO2 in the electrolyte increases the specific charge by at least 25% relative to that obtained using the reference cell with standard electrolyte.
· Starting from the second cycle (after the irreversible transition from α-S8 to β-S8), the specific charge is ∼1,080 mAh g-1 for the cell with fumed SiO2 as an electrolyte additive, and only ∼870 mAh g-1 for the cell cycled without it (Fig. 7).
· The Coulombic eﬃciency of almost 90% is achieved when SiO2 is used as an additive, whereas in the case of the reference cell with the standard electrolyte, the Coulombic eﬃciency barely reaches 78%.

· The higher viscosity and the lower conductivity of the LiTFSI- based electrolyte result in the higher polarization compared with the LiFSI-based one,33 leading to the larger overpotential for LiTFSI-mediated lamellar Li2S growth.
· The salt-mediated Li2S growth determines the primary growth plane, which presents (200) in the LiTFSI-based electrolyte while (220) in the LiFSI-based one confirmed by TEM and XRD measure- ments.
· Covalent Li-C bonds can be formed between Li2S film and graphene,34,35 promoting the deposition of the subsequent Li2S molecules and the planar growth of lamellar Li2S in LiTFSI-based electrolyte.
· In LiFSI-based electrolyte, the Li2S (220) is a more stable surface structure around the cell voltage of Li-S battery as reported previously.36,37 This is in favor of the 3D growth of spherical Li2S.
· DFT-based theoretical simulations were built to attempt to figure out the salt eﬀects of LiTFSI and LiFSI on deposited Li2S, as shown in Figure S10.
· Further theoretical analysis of the salt-mediated growth mode, as a follow-up work, should be carried out in the presence of the two salts based on a more optimized model in the actual electrolyte environment for deep understanding of the nucleation and growth of Li2S. 2.
· The interfacial structure and dynamic transformation are strongly correlated with the battery performance, which is critical for the radical understanding and designing of advanced batteries with optimized electrolyte.
· This volume diﬀerence is inherent to the structures of sphere and lamella, which might be one of the important factors explaining the larger initial capacity of the Li-S battery in LiFSI-based electrolyte.33 Besides that, the reversibility of the spherical and lamellar Li2S is compared upon charging under the same circ…
· Accordingly, the combination of the lamellar and spherical Li2S on cathode can take advantage of both of them, leading to the relatively high reversibility and capacity of the Li-S batteries in LiTFSI-LiFSI binary-salt electrolyte in contrast to those in individual-salt electrolytes (Figure S13).
· In addition, roles of the Li salts in mediating the kinetics of the Li-S electrochemistry are further elucidated and compared by the CV analyses as shown in Figure S14.
· The b values in LiTFSI, LiFSI, and LiTFSI-LiFSI binary-salt environments are 0.87, 0.92, and 1.02, respectively, indicating that the kinetics is transferring from a mix-controlled reaction to the surface reaction governed one.
· This variation can be attributed to the change of Li-S interactions and electrolyte viscosity in diﬀerent Li salt electrolytes.
· The TFSI-anions added in the electrolyte can interact actively with Li ions and weaken the Li-S networks in sulfides, accelerating the surface reaction as reported recently.42 In LiTFSI-LiFSI binary-salt electrolyte, the higher ionic conductivity with lower viscosity38 is beneficial for the Li ion diﬀusion and promotes…
· The Li ions in LiFSI-based electrolyte can coordinate with not only O but also F atoms from the solvents and salts,10 indicating the weaker Li-S networks16 and the further accelerated surface reaction.
· However, the increased ionic conductivity and the decreased viscosity of the LiFSI-based electrolyte accelerate the diﬀusion as well.38 Thus, both the interfacial reaction and the diﬀusion act and mediate the reaction kinetics in LiFSI- based electrolyte.
· As shown in Figure S15b, c, the b values of the short-chain sulfide reactions (peaks 2 and 3) decrease in these three electrolytes, indicating that the kinetics is impeded by the slow solid-state diﬀusion.
· Because of the strong Li-S network and less interaction with the solvent molecules, the short-chain sulfides tend to exist as dimers or clusters in electrolyte.43 The solid-state reaction nature limits the Li ion diﬀusion during the short-chain sulfide transitions, resulting in the decline of the b values. 4.
· CONCLUSION In conclusion, via in-situ AFM monitoring, we have successfully disclosed the interfacial mechanism of the impact of Li salts on the dynamic evolution of the Li-S electro- chemical reactions.
· It is discovered that Li salts play a key role in governing the growth pathways of Li2S at nanoscale, which determine the corresponding deposition morphology and decomposition modes upon discharge/charge.
· Schematic illustration of the sulfide reactions at the cathode-electrolyte interfaces in LiTFSI-LiFSI binary-salt electro- lyte.
· In comparison, LiFSI salt favors a 3D growth mode to form spherical Li2S during deposition, taking center-to-edge mode and generating hollow spheres during decomposition.
· The Li salts mediate the interfacial structure and dynamic processes of the sulfides on cathodes, which subsequently influence the Li-S cell perform- ance.
· In addition, the Li salts can further manipulate the viscosity, conductivity, and Li-S, Li-solvent interactions in electrolyte, governing the distinct Li-S kinetics of the multistep polysulfide reactions under diﬀerent salt environ- ments.
· These investigations reveal the precise control of the Li salts over the Li-S electrochemical reactions via real-time monitoring at nanoscale, providing the deep understanding of the interfacial mechanisms and opening a fundamentally new perspective for rational designing of Li-S batteries with high- energy density and…

· Conclusions We reported that a signicantly increased amount of PEO-like polymers were obtained in the SEI lms of cycled Li electrodes by introducing polysulde into a carbonate electrolyte, which resulted in the inhibition of the formation of Li dendrites.
· The polysulde likely acts as a catalyst and is not consumed during repeated charging/discharging cycles, and thus, the long-cycle stability of lithium-metal batteries can be remarkably improved.
· An extremely small amount of the polysulde added into the carbonate electrolyte can trigger a series of chain reac- tions to generate PEO-like polymers and LiF during cycling, which are relatively stable species.
· The Li deposits on the surface of Li electrodes cycled in the polysulde/carbonate electrolyte exhibited a dense structure and a smooth surface even aer 300 cycles under a current density of 1 mA cm2.
· The fundamental ndings revealed in this work may open a new avenue for designing Li-metal battery electrolytes and possible applications of polysulde in polymer electrolytes.

#### P090 | 10.1149/2.0161801jes | Electrolyte Decomposition and Electrode Thickness Changes in Li-S Cells with Lithium Metal Anodes, Prelithiated Silicon Anodes and Hard Carbon Anodes
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=N 因素=负极类型为主；LiNO3 有无 Ⅱ=Y 备注=锂离子–硫负极为主要变量

· Journal of The Electrochemical Society OPEN ACCESS Electrolyte Decomposition and Electrode Thickness Changes in Li-S Cells with Lithium Metal Anodes, Prelithiated Silicon Anodes and Hard Carbon Anodes To cite this article: K.
· Low electrolyte/sulfur mass ratios or more generallly, the relative amount of electrolyte in a Li-S cell have an especially high impact on the achievable gravimetric energy density.
· A negative side effect of low electrolyte/sulfur ratios are low cycle numbers due to electrolyte decomposition and the possibility that electrolyte becomes inaccessible at the lithium metal anode when the lithium becomes more and more porous during cycling.
· The thickness measurements also helped to explain different electrolyte decomposition behavior and they can be used to discuss the impact of thickness changes on gas analysis.
· The electrolyte decomposition of the Li-S standard electrolyte based on lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI) in dimethoxyethane (DME): dioxolane (DIOX) with LiNO3 was examined by online mass spectrometry (MS) within Li-S, Li-HC, Li-Si, LiSi-S and LiHC-S cells.
· Several electrolyte decomposition products were verified by post-mortem gas chromatography (GC) and gas chromatography-mass spectrometry (GC-MS) of Li-S cells with cycled electrolyte. © The Author(s) 2017.
· Despite all this, the electrolyte is a major weight source in Li-S cells even if the electrolyte/sulfur weight ratio (E/S) is low.
· With the Li-S standard electrolyte based on lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI) in dimethoxyethane (DME): dioxolane (DOL) with LiNO3 additive, the lowest obtainable E/S ratio is likely >3:1.
· This is due to the high porosity of the sulfur cathode (usually ∼60-80%) which has to be filled with electrolyte and because of the reaction mechanism.
· With the standard electrolyte ac- tive sulfur is transported out of the cathode in the form of polysulfides which have a high impact on the electrolytes viscosity and resistance.
· If the polysulfide concentration is too high the discharge typically stops at or shortly after the voltage dip between higher and lower voltage plateau when the electrolyte resistance shows its maximum.3 The total electrolyte amount and the E/S ratio have a high impact on the obtainable cycle number.
· A low E/S is the basic requirement to obtain Li-ion level gravimetric energy densities but reduces the obtainable cycle number to only a few dozen cycles.
· This is due to electrolyte decomposition and the fact that the SEI on the lithium zE-mail: markus.hagen@ict.fraunhofer.de metal anode has to be repaired or enhanced each time fresh lithium gets deposited or when lithium becomes more and more porous/mossy.
· Additionally pores and voids in the lithium metal anode generated during cycling can trap electrolyte and therefore might require that the available electrolyte amount be higher in aged cells compared to fresh assembled cells.
· To increase the obtainable Li-S cycle number the electrolyte must be as stable as possible and the cell setup and electrolyte must enable a smooth, dense lithium deposition.
· The aim of this work is to provide deeper insights into elec- trolyte decomposition in Li-S cells with standard electrolyte depen- dent whether a lithium metal, a prelithiated silicon or a prelithiated carbon anode were applied.
· To examine the electrolyte decomposi- tion online mass spectrometry during cycling was performed.
· The role of the popular LiNO3 additive was examined by testing electrolytes with and without LiNO3.
· Solvent-free sulfur cathodes were prepared by hot pressing 60% sulfur, 30% Ketjen Black EC-600 JD (Akzonobel) and 10% PEO binder (Acros Organics) on a carbon coated aluminum current collector.
· Electrolyte.—As electrolyte 0.7 M LiTFSI in DME:DOL (2:1, v:v) (BASF) was applied and 0.25 M LiNO3 (ABCR 99.98%; metals ba- sis) was added if required.
· Differing salt and/or LiNO3 concentrations or solvent ratios might change the gaseous or liquid decomposition products during/after cycling.
· Gas chromatography and gas chromatography mass spectrometry.—The liquid electrolyte - withdrawn after the cy- cling experiments - was analyzed by a combination of a Headspace Autosampler (PerkinElmer TurboMatrix) connected to a PerkinElmer Clarus 600 GC-MS system.
· The second path is capable of separating compounds with higher polarity (like solvents of LIBs and their degradation products) and up to four detectors are connected to this separation path.
· The MID scans were normalized to a high intensity electrolyte background signal to remove the effect of pres- sure on signal intensity.
· LiTFSI in DME:DOL + LiNO3 was applied in all corresponding tests except the Samsung S7 Li-ion cell.
· Still it has to be considered that Walu´s et al. applied a sulfone based electrolyte with pressure free thickness measurement in a commercial type cell and the here reported measurements were done at ∼1 kg cm-2 with DME:DOL electrolyte and electrolyte to sulfur mass ratios of ∼7:1.
· Very likely the applied electrolyte has the most significant impact on the thickness and especially the lithium deposition.
· Mikhaylik et al.12 reported a complete lithium depletion after 60-80 cycles with DME:DOL based electrolyte.

· In-situ electrolyte examinations - online mass spectrometry.— Mass spectrometry can be used to relate the mass/charge (m/z) sig- nals of electrolyte and electrode decomposition products to the cell voltage, current and state-of-charge.

· A main target is to evaluate and to benchmark methods, materials and especially electrolytes which show a lower or ideally no decomposition.

· Normalizing to a high intensity electrolyte signal has the advantage that the amount of electrolyte in a cell is relatively constant with a high initial excess of electrolyte and comparably low examined cycle numbers.

· The applied sulfur cathode was created with water as a solvent during slurry creation.
· It is likely that there is some water contamination of the electrolyte with a possible impact on the gas analysis measurement.
· Post-mortem electrolyte analysis - GCMS.—As discussed pre- viously, it can be difficult to identify specific substances with only online MS.
· To prove specific electrolyte decomposition products ex- situ gas chromatography in combination with mass spectrometry was used.
· Several electrolyte samples were analyzed to determine the con- tents, with particular emphasis placed on whether the m/z 31, 58, and 76 signals observed during in-situ MS analysis could be methanol, methoxy-ethene and CS2.
· It must be noted that solvent based sul- fur cathodes have high concentrations of remaining solvent.
· The sulfur cathodes in this work which were created with water as a solvent are therefore likely to have significant water impurities.
· The FID and GC-MS analysis of fresh electrolyte including LiNO3 can be seen in Figs. 11a, 11b obviously proving that the applied elec- trolyte indeed contains DME and DOL as well as glove box argon.
· When soaked into a sulfur cathode (no cycling), the electrolyte GC analysis showed some additional peaks from the fresh electrolyte.
· The presence of methanol and ethanol suggests that the electrolyte reacted upon contact with the sulfur cathode.
· When cycled in a symmetrical Li-Li cell from 0-3 V at 5 mV/s with LiNO3 the electrolyte was found to contain ethanol, methanol and a new compound, methoxy-ethene.
· Figs. 12a, 12b shows the comparison of the mass spectrum observed, at a retention time of 4.5 minutes (see Fig. 13b), during GC-MS electrolyte analysis vs. the NIST mass spectrum for methoxy-ethene (CAS 107-25-5).
· Similarly, GC analysis of electrolyte without LiNO3 when cycled in a symmetrical Li-Li cell from 0-3 V at 5 mV s-1, showed the presence of methoxy-ethene but methanol and ethanol were not detected.
· Electrolyte with and without LiNO3 cycled in symmetrical Li-Li cells showed no evidence of compounds relating to the suspected CS2.
· This finding suggests that the presence of CS2, as seen in the cell cycling MS analysis, is due to an electrochemical reaction between the electrolyte and the cathode during cycling and does not corre- spond to decomposition of TFSI (the only source of sulfur in Li-Li tests).
· The GC analysis of Li-S cells cycled from 1.0-3.0 V at 0.1 mV s-1 with and without LiNO3 provides further insight into the origin of the suspected electrolyte decomposition products from the in-situ MS analysis of cell cycling.
· In Li-S cells cycled with LiNO3 additive, methanol, ethanol and methoxy-ethene were again detected in addi- tion to a small amount of acetone.
· Since methanol, ethanol, and methoxy-ethene were found in a Li-Li cell with LiNO3 containing electrolyte, it suggests that the presence of the sulfur cathode does not have an influence on this decompo- sition mechanism.
· Electrolyte without LiNO3, cycled in a Li-S cell also showed the presence of methanol, ethanol, and methoxy-ethene.
· Therefore the presence of ethanol and methanol must be related to both the interaction of electrolyte with the cathode and the presence of LiNO3.
· One of the most important results obtained from the GC analysis of Li-S cells was the presence of CS2 confirmed by the analysis of a CS2 standard for both electrolytes with and without LiNO3.
· The MS signal from GC-MS for the 6.7 min retention time peak (see Fig. 13c), associated with CS2, in electrolyte with LiNO3 cycled in a Li-S cell can be seen in Figs. 12c, 12d compared to the NIST database spectrum for CS2.
· The qualitative GC-MS analysis of gaseous phase in Li-S cell revealed two intensive signals for the electrolyte components DME and DOL.
· Overview of selected MS m/z signals for a LiSi-S cell cycled with electrolyte containing LiNO3 between 1.2-2.6 V.

· Generation of hydrogen and nitrogen - especially during the ini- tial open circuit voltage (OCV) phases and the first charge/discharge steps - is also described by Schneider et al.7 and Jozwiuk et al.8 Ad- ditionally, the appearance of N2O for diglyme/LiTFSI as electrolyte is described, which could not be detected here…
· Evolving of N2O is assigned to the degradation of LiNO3, which is also added to the elec- trolyte in our experiments.
· Other degradation products like methanol and ethanol found for the diglyme/LiTFSI electrolyte system8 are also not found here, despite a sensitive GC-MS system being applied.
· Devia- tions between the published values and the relative amounts calculated here might be due to differences in the source of electrode material and the ratio of electrolyte components used.
· Online mass spectrometry (MS) is a powerful tool to evaluate the stability of an electrolyte.
· For the liquid electrolyte phase methoxy-ethene, CS2 and short chain alcohols could be veri- fied by Headspace-GC-MS.
· This could mean that the increasing porosity of the lithium metal anode has a higher impact on the obtainable cycle number compared to the electrolyte decomposition keeping the much higher cycle number of LiHC-S cells in mind.
· As an overall conclusion, the Li-S standard electrolyte based on DME:DOL solvents and LiNO3 additive can be regarded as unsta- ble for most systems and should be substituted with more stable alternatives if high energy densities and long cycle life are to be achieved.

· Direct Visualization of Lithium Polysulfides and Their Suppression in Liquid Electrolyte Hyeon Kook Seo, Yoon Hwa, Joon Ha Chang, Jae Yeol Park, Jae Sang Lee, Jungjae Park, Elton J.
· However, it suﬀers from the lack of direct observation on behaviors of soluble Li-PS in liquid electrolytes.
· We found that the morphological changes and Li-PS diﬀusion are retarded by ionic liquid (IL) addition into electrolyte.
· Chronoamperometric shuttle current measurement confirms that IL addition lowers the experimental diﬀusion coeﬃcient of Li-PS by 2 orders of magnitude relative to that in IL-free electrolyte and thus suppresses the Li-PS shuttle current, which accounts for better cyclability and Coulombic eﬃciency of the Li/S cell.
· This study provides significant insights into electrolyte design to inhibit the polysulfide shuttle phenomenon.

· According to Fick’s first law, the flux of the diﬀusing species (Li- PS in the electrolytes in this case) is determined by the diﬀusion coeﬃcient and initial concentration of Li-PS in the electrolyte and they strongly depend on the viscosity and Li-PS solubility of the electrolyte.
· Thus, it is expected that the addition of IL to the liquid electrolyte can mitigate Li-PS shuttle due to its low Li-PS solubility and high viscosity.
· To electrochemically verify our hypothesis accounting for the eﬀect of the IL-containing electrolyte, the Li-PS shuttle current of the Li/S cells with the IL-containing electrolyte (50% of IL by volume) and the IL-free electrolyte was chronoamperometri- cally measured.
· As shown in Figure 1a, the IL-containing electrolyte cell showed almost no current for 40 h with negligible charge obtained (17 mAh/gS, Figure 1b), whereas the IL-free electrolyte cell exhibited a significant shuttle current (7-10 µA) that corresponded to a specific capacity of 430 mAh/gS, (Figure 1c).

· After overlapping the two graphene-coated TEM grids with facing graphene-coated sides each other, 20 µL of the sulfur nanoparticle-dispersed electrolyte solution (5 mg of sulfur nanoparticles dispersed in 1 mL of the LiTFSI-based IL-free or the IL-containing electrolyte) is drop-cast onto the overlapped TEM grids.

· In summary, with the in situ GLC-EM technique, we visualized the lithiation dynamics of sulfur nanoparticles in liquid electrolytes in real time and demonstrated the eﬀect of the IL on the formation and diﬀusion of Li-PS during the lithiation process.
· During the lithiation process of sulfur nanoparticles in the conventional IL-free electrolyte, the morphology of sulfur nanoparticles changes from spherical to irregular due to the formation and dissolution of Li-PS.
· In contrast, the sulfur nanoparticle in the IL-containing electrolyte maintains its pristine morphology during the lithiation process because the low solubility of Li-PS and high viscosity of the IL-containing electrolyte prevent the formation and diﬀusion of Li-PS.
· The results of electrochemical Li-PS shuttle current measurements are in accordance with the in situ GLC-EM observation, where the Li-PS shuttle was successfully inhibited by the presence of the IL in the electrolyte and an approximately 2 orders of magnitude lower experimental diﬀusion coeﬃcient was obtained for the I…
· Consequently, the Li/S cell employing the IL- containing electrolyte exhibited improved cyclability and Coulombic eﬃciency over 100 cycles than the IL-free electrolyte cell.

· The eﬀect of such coating on the surface properties and electrochemistry of lithium sulfide cathodes is investigated in details, in particular, regarding its interaction with fresh vs. aged electrolyte.
· It was found that upon prolonged storage (12+ months) the dioxolane-based electrolyte tends to polymerize, which greatly aﬀects the behavior of Li2S-based cathodes.
· The 1:1 volume ratio mixture of Dimethoxyethane (DME, Solvionic) and 1,3-Dioxolane (DOL, Solvionic) was used as the electrolyte solvent.
· Prior to mixing, the two solvents were individually dried using 3Å molecular sieves (Sigma Aldrich) to reach water contents below 20 ppm as determined via Karl-Fischer titration (Mettler-Toledo Titrator Compact C30).
· Appropriate amounts of lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI, Solvionic) and lithium nitrate (LiNO3, Alfa Aesar) were dissolved into the DME-DOL solvent mixture to achieve concentration of 1 and 0.25 mol L-1, respectively, using aluminum bottles.
· This electrolyte was used soon after its preparation (i.e., within 2 weeks; labeled as “fresh”) or after storage (i.e., after more than 1 year, labeled “aged”).
· Besides the aging, both the electrolytes were stored in sealed aluminum bottle inside the glove box.
· For the determination of the electrolyte electrochemical stability window, carbon electrodes composed of Super C65 and PVdF were prepared in the 85:15 weight ratio.
· The cells were assembled using 100 µL of electrolyte soaked into glass fiber separators (Whatman GF/A).
· The anodic electrochemical stability of the electrolytes was studied by cyclic voltammetry (CV), where the potential was scanned from 3 to 4 V vs.
· After cycling, each cell was disassembled inside a glove box, and the electrolyte recovered by centrifuging the glass fiber separators at 6,000 rpm for 10 min.
· The recovered electrolytes were analyzed via ATR-IR.
· RESULTS AND DISCUSSION Figure 1A shows the capacity retention of Li2S-CC-based electrodes when using either the fresh (Li2S-CC_F) or the aged (Li2S-CC_A) electrolyte.
· It is evident that the electrolyte aging has a large impact on the cell performance.
· Surprisingly, the aged electrolyte provides higher discharge capacity as well as capacity retention upon 100 cycles.
· In order to better understand the reason behind this eﬀect, the discharge curves of the cathode in each electrolyte were closely examined.

· The formation of polyDOL in the aged electrolyte is proven by the increase in the band at around 1,190 cm-1, which is caused by the vibrations of the C-O bonds in linear molecules (Yoshida and Matsuura, 1998).
· Analysis of the pure solvent mixture (1:1 DME:DOL) without addition of salt and stored under the same conditions for the same period of time shows no change in its spectrum, such that the culprit is likely the salts added to form the electrolyte (LiNO3 and LiTFSI).
· In summary, the polymerization of DOL occurring upon prolonged storage of the electrolyte appears to be beneficial in Li2S-CC_A cell, which shows a higher capacity than the Li2S-CC_F cell (Figure 1A) mainly due to a higher and more stable capacity delivered in region II (Figure 1C).

· It is rather challenging to reduce the amount of electrolyte in Li  S cells because the discharge reactions of sulfur (Li2Sx formation: x=8  1) occur via a fully dissolution/precipitation conversion mechanism in conventional electrolyte solutions.
· Therefore, the use of sparingly solvating electrolytes has been reported as an effective method to reduce the electrolyte content in Li  S cells.
· However, the majority of related research to date has been based on the use of an excess amount of electrolyte and low S loading.
· In this study, we investigated the performance of Li  S cells using cathodes with a relatively high S loading (>4 mgcm  2) under lean electrolyte conditions of sparingly solvating electrolytes.
· The inhomogeneous discharge reactions could be alleviated by enhancing the transport properties of the electro- lyte and adequately maintaining the porous structure of the cathode by incorporating an additive in it.

· Initially, by employing a sparingly solvating G4-based electrolyte with a low Li+ transport capability, we demonstrate that the actual mass transport in a Li  S cell is not only determined by the intrinsic properties of the electrolyte itself, but also by the properties of the sulfur cathode.
· In addition, we demonstrate the TiB additive-induced enhancement of the mass transport in the sulfur cathode using a sparingly solvating SL- based electrolyte with a high Li+ transport capability.
· Ultimately, the aim of this study is to clarify the key factors that influence the performance of a Li  S battery consisting of a cathode with a high sulfur loading under lean electrolyte conditions.

#### P254 | 10.1016/j.chempr.2022.07.004 | Cationic lithium polysulfides in lithium-sulfur batteries
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=C 因素=锂盐浓度对阳离子型多硫化物的影响（机理为主） Ⅱ=N 备注=存疑：电池性能比较是否成立待核

· However, the existing form of LiPSs in electrolyte has not yet been clearly eluci- dated.
· Kinetic evaluations further indicate that cationic LiPSs are disadvantageous to battery performance, and accordingly, decreasing the Li salt concentration can inhibit the formation of cationic LiPSs and improve the energy density of practical Li-S pouch cells.
· Revealing the existing form of LiPSs in electrolyte is essential for understanding the reaction mechanism and rationally designing high-performance Li-S batteries.
· Herein, we pioneeringly demonstrate that LiPSs are weakly dissociated in electrolyte but exhibit a strong tendency to bond extra lithium ions and form cationic LiPSs (e.g., Li3S6 +).
· Accordingly, decreasing the salt concen- tration is demonstrated to inhibit the formation of cationic LiPSs and is manifested effective to improve the performance of practical high- energy-density Li-S pouch cells.
· This work reveals the existence of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte and affords directional guidance on improving the electrochemical performance of Li-S batteries.
· In this report, we reveal for the first time that LiPSs exhibit a strong tendency to bond extra lithium ions and form cationic clusters, implying that cationic LiPSs exist as the main species in electrolyte of Li-S batteries.
· These findings update the basic understanding on Li-S battery chemistry and inspire further rational design of electrolyte and electrode materials for construct- ing high-performance batteries.
· Chem 8, 3031-3050, November 10, 2022 ª 2022 Elsevier Inc. 3031 ll improving the LiPS solubility by introducing high-donor-number solvents promotes the LiPS reactivity at the cathode, thus affording high discharge capacities.16,17 On the contrary, solvents such as hydrofluoroethers18,19 and di-isopropyl sulfide20 exhibi…
· In addition, several ionic additives such as ammonium cation,21 tet- rabutylammonium cation,22 and trifluoroacetate anion23 have been manifested to regulate the formation of LiPS clusters or stabilize polysulfide radicals for kinetic improvements.
· Despite the remarkable progress in regulating the solubility and solvation structure of LiPSs, the actual existing form of LiPSs in electrolyte has not yet been revealed unambiguously, especially the dissociation and association behavior between lithium ions (Li+) and polysulfide anions (Sn 2, n = 4, 6, or 8).
· In other words, whether and how the dissolved Li2Sn molecules are ionized and associated with surrounding electrolyte species remain controversial.
· Anionic LiSn  and Sn 2 are typically weakly solvated so that the rearrangement of the sulfur chains is suggested as the key limiting factor,29,30 whereas neutral Li2Sn associated with additional Li+ will be more strongly solvated, and the energy barrier of desolva- tion or Li-S bond rearrangement might become more si…
· In this contribution, the existence of cationic LiPSs is experimentally identified in electrolyte of Li-S batteries for the first time.
· A combination of electron spray ionization mass spec- trometry (ESI-MS), 7Li NMR spectroscopy, and theoretical calculations further proves that LiPSs prefer to bond extra Li+ provided by additional Li salts to form well- defined cationic LiPSs.
· As a result, an optimization strategy of decreasing the salt concentration to reduce the number of cationic LiPS is proposed and manifested to be effective in practical high-energy-density Li-S pouch cells.
· This work indicates the existence of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte of Li-S batteries and provides directional guidance on the performance promotion of Li-S batteries.
· RESULTS AND DISCUSSION Dissociation and association behavior of LiPS in electrolyte Conductivity analysis was firstly conducted to determine the dissociation behavior of LiPSs.
· The solvent was mixed 1,3-dioxolane (DOL) and DME with equal volume.

· The Kd value is fitted to be 1:69310 4 and corresponds to a weak dissociation behavior of Li2S6 in electrolyte.
· The triple-ion ioniza- tion behavior is considered to be universal in various solvent-salt systems as re- ported by other observations.48 For the triple-ion process, the reaction equation of Li2S6 with the corresponding triple-ion association equilibrium constant is assumed as follows: Li2S6!1  3 Li3S + 6 + 1  3 Li3S…
· The calculated Ka value is relatively high as 1.09, implying the triple- ion association behavior is significant in practical LiPS electrolyte with high concen- tration of LiPSs.
· Accordingly, the major species in Li2S6 electrolyte with different concentrations can be recognized.
· Identification of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte Essentially, the triple-ion-association behavior is caused by one Li2S6 molecule that obtains a Li+ from another Li2S6 molecule.
· Therefore, it is deduced that if there is a ll 3034 Chem 8, 3031-3050, November 10, 2022 Article certain amount of free Li+ ion in electrolyte, the Li2S6 molecules can bond with Li+ spontaneously and exist in the form of cationic Li3S6 +.
· With extra lithium bis(trifluorome- thanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI, a typical Li salt widely used in Li-S batteries) added to the Li2S6 solution, the intensity of the Li3S6 + peak is significantly increased, whereas the peaks of LiS6 /S6  in the negative mode almost disappear.
· Moreover, 7Li NMR spin-lattice (t1) relaxation measurements were carried out at var- ied temperatures to probe the existence of cationic Li3S6 + in electrolyte.
· Identification of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte (A and B) (A) ESI-MS spectra of Li2S6 with or without LiTFSI and (B) corresponding peak intensity of LiS6 , S6 , and Li3S6 + species.
· As shown in Figure S3, 100 mM LiTFSI exhibits a conductivity of 799 mS cm1 in DOL/DME mixed solvent, and Li2S6 with the concentrations of 20, 40, 60, or 80 mM exhibits conductivities of 40.2, 94.2, 164, or 251 mS cm1, respectively.
· The reaction equation can be written as follows: Li2S6 + LiTFSI!Li3S + 6 + TFSI : Obviously, the results of conductivity measurements confirm that Li2S6 has a strong tendency to bond an additional Li+ and form a considerable amount of cationic Li3S6 + in the electrolyte with Li salts.
· With the addition of Li salts, the dissociation and association reactions between Li2S6 and Li+ are quantitatively elucidated in Figure 3B.
· The large amount of free Li+ cations provided by LiTFSI leads to a dominant fraction of above 80% of cationic Li3S6 + species in the electrolyte when the Li2S6 concentration is below 100 mM.
· Therefore, for the Li-S cells with excess electrolyte usage (electrolyte to sulfur ratio, E/S ratio > 20 mL g1), cationic LiPSs are considered the major species in electrolyte.
· For the high-energy-density Li-S cells with limited electrolyte (E/S ratio < 5 mL g1), both neutral LiPS molecules and LiPS cations exist as the major species in electrolyte.
· Theoretical analysis of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte The existence of cationic LiPSs has been comprehensively confirmed by experi- mental methods.
· (B) Dissociation and association reactions of Li2S6 with the existence of Li salts and corresponding equilibrium constants.
· (C) Simulated fraction of different sulfur species with 1.0 M LiTFSI at different Li2S6 concentrations. ll Chem 8, 3031-3050, November 10, 2022 3037 Article electrolyte and understand the underlying chemistry, first-principles calculations were conducted.
· Concretely, the formation of LiPS cations in electrolyte is consid- ered with both implicit and explicit solvation models.
· Theoretical simulation of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte (A-C) Optimized structure of (A) Li3S4 +-(DME)2, (B) Li3S6 +-(DME)2, and (C) Li3S8 +-(DME)2 from DFT calculations.
· MD simulations were further carried out to investigate the solvation structure of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte.
· Figures 4C-4E exhibit the RDF and CN of the electrolytes with Li2S4, Li2S6, or Li2S8, respectively.
· The Sn 2-Sn 2 RDF demonstrates no peak within 6 A˚ distances, indicating no LiPS aggregation at 1.0 M salt concentration.
· Therefore, the results of MD simulations illustrate the general existence of LiPS cations in electrolyte.
· In summary, DFT calculations and MD simulations reveal the binding energy, stable configuration, and solvation structure of the LiPS cations, thus theoretically confirm- ing the existence of LiPS cations in electrolyte which agrees with experimental results.
· Kinetic evaluation on cationic LiPSs As is discussed above, the dissolved LiPSs exist in the forms of cations rather than neutral molecules or anions in Li-S electrolyte with Li salts.
· The reactivity of the cationic LiPSs with Li metal anode was also evaluated, consid- ering that the reaction between LiPSs and Li metal anodes significantly affects the cycling lifespan of Li-S batteries.53 Shuttle current was employed to quantitatively reflect the reaction rate between the dissolved LiPSs in electroly…
· Therefore, a strategy of decreasing the Li salt concentration to reduce the cationic LiPS fraction is proposed to improve the battery performance.
· In contrast, reducing the LiTFSI concentration to 50 mM decreases the proportion of cationic LiPSs in electrolyte so as to improve the redox kinetics at the cathode.
· To further explore the feasibility of the proposed strategy for practical applications, 2 Ah level Li-S pouch cells were assembled with the same electrolyte amount but different LiTFSI concentrations.
· The cathode areal sulfur loading was 7.3 mg cm2, the anode was 75 mm-thick Li foil, and the E/S ratio was controlled to be 3.5 g gS 1.
· The LiTFSI concentration was 0.20 M, whereas conventional 1.0 M LiTFSI electrolyte was set as a control.
· (D) Voltage-capacity profiles of Li-S pouch cells with different LiTFSI concentrations. ll 3042 Chem 8, 3031-3050, November 10, 2022 Article to form LiPS cations, and cationic LiPSs are identified to be the dominant species in electrolyte with Li salts.
· Accordingly, decreasing the Li salt concentration to reduce the proportion of LiPS cations is manifested to efficiently enhance the battery performances regarding capacities, polarization, and rate re- sponses.
· More importantly, following the strategy, Li-S pouch cells achieve a high actual energy density of 353 Wh kg1 with a low Li salt concentration.
· The findings on the existing form and behavior of LiPSs provide new insights on the Li-S chemistry and afford important guidance on rational design of electrolyte and electrode mate- rials for constructing high-performance Li-S batteries.

· Meini et al. firstly explored metallocenes as redox mediators, which can eﬀectively reduce the delithiation overpotential of Li 2 S, thereby promoting the oxidation reaction of Li 2 S [14] .
· Similarly, most of the extrinsic redox mediators can only regulate a relatively sin- gle electrochemical reaction step, while the electrochemical reactions at other potentials still remain poor kinetics [ 15 , 16 ].
· Yu). 1 These authors contributed equally to this work. dox mediator should reversibly interact with polysulfides and possess stronger redox regulation capabilities for the overall electrochemical process [17-19] .
· As redox mediators of LSBs, SMOCs could attend the electrochemical reactions of polysulfides and improve their corresponding kinetics, achieving high electrochemical performances [21] .
· However, most of SMOCs are in liquid state and could dissolve in electrolytes, causing the shuttle eﬀect and the instability of the elec- trochemical processes [22] .
· Recently, polymer organosulfur compounds (POCs) have been reported to react reversibly with polysulfides, making them promising as novel polysulfide redox mediators [ 23 , 24 ].

#### P273 | 10.1021/acsaem.2c01459 | Homogeneous Organometallic Catalysts for Improved Electrochemical Kinetics of Li-S Batteries
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=M 因素=二茂钛均相催化剂（以 DFT 为主） Ⅱ=N 备注=存疑：电池实验是否成立待核

· Homogeneous Organometallic Catalysts for Improved Electrochemical Kinetics of Li-S Batteries Rahul Jayan, Md Shahriar Nahian, and Md Mahbubul Islam* Cite This: ACS Appl.
· Energy Mater. 2022, 5, 9782-9790 Read Online ACCESS Metrics & More Article Recommendations ABSTRACT: The dissolution of polysulfides into electrolytes and sluggish electro- chemical conversion kinetics primarily impede the practical realization of Li-S batteries.
· Homogeneous catalysis is an effective strategy to overcome the challenges involved under lean electrolyte conditions.
· In this study, we used first-principles density functional theory (DFT) simulations to understand the role of metallocenes (using titanocene (TiCp2) as an archetypical example) as homogeneous catalysts in the electrolyte medium to suppress the shuttle effect and promote the reaction kinetics.
· The calculated electrochemical stability window of TiCp2 reveals that the composition is electrochemically inactive in the operating potential range of Li-S batteries and can thus be leveraged as an additive to expedite the reduction kinetics of soluble lithium polysulfides (LiPSs).
· We studied the detailed characteristic behavior of LiPS interactions with TiCp2 in both gas and solvent phases and the kinetics of elementary sulfur reduction reactions (SRRs).
· We further propose a mechanistic scheme of TiCp2 homogeneous catalyst- boosted sulfur redox cycles.
· Overall, our simulations predicted moderate binding and improved kinetics of polysulfide chemistry with the metallocene-based homogeneous catalyst and are expected to lead to a paradigm shift in the design of organometallic additives for achieving high-performance metal-sulfur batteries.
· KEYWORDS: homogeneous catalyst, additive, titanocene, shuttle effect, DFT 1.
· For a competitive energy density and low cost of LiSBs, reduction of the electrolyte volume to at least E/S < 5 is essential.24 However, lowering the E/S ratio inevitably results in adverse conditions in Li-S batteries.
· First, they exhibit weak reversibility and poor sulfur reaction kinetics because of the low quantity of the polysulfide redox mediator for the transition from higher-order LiPSs to lower-order ones and significant polarization.
· Second, most carbon-based cathode materials generally require a higher volume of electrolyte to moisten the cathode surface.
· Although there are a significant number of studies on metallocenes as redox mediators for LiSBs, there exists a wide knowledge gap concerning the development of homogeneous metallocene-based additives to promote the sulfur conversion kinetics under lean electrolyte conditions.
· Herein, for the first time, we use metallocenes (titanocene (TiCp2) as an archetypical example) as functional additives to suppress the shuttle effect and enhance the electrochemical performance of LiSBs.
· The TiCp2 homogeneous catalyst exhibits a moderate binding strength toward various LiPS species and acts as a catalyst to expedite the rate-determining steps in sulfur reduction reactions (SRR) over the discharging process.
· On the basis of insights obtained from the adsorption energy calculations, we proposed a mechanistic scheme for the sulfur redox cycle facilitated by the homogeneous TiCp2 catalyst.
· On the other hand, upon charging, TiCp2 rebinds with DME in the electrolyte because of the much lower affinity of TiCp2 toward S8 compared with DME, and the sulfur redox cycles continue to occur during the charge-discharge cycles. 2.
· To account for the solvent effect, the COSMO-SMD implicit solvation model was used considering diethyl ether as the medium with a dielectric constant of ε = 4.5, which is close to the dielectric constants of DME and 1,3- dioxolane (DOL).48,49 We calculated Gibbs free energies at a temperature of 298 K using the procedu…
· Redox mediators are a class of homogeneous catalysts whose electrochemical potentials are carefully matched with the sulfur conversion reaction potentials.42,43 In contrast to redox mediators, to work as electrolyte additives, homogeneous catalysts for LiSBs must have an inactive electrochemical redox behavior toward t…
· It is evident from the results that TiCp2 undergoes single-electron reduction outside the operating window of the LiSB (1.8- 2.8 V vs Li/Li+), an important criterion for the design of electrolyte additives.
· The optimized geometric configurations of LiPSs and their bond lengths were analyzed in our previous study.18 To elucidate the binding behavior of bent titanocene for efficient homogeneous catalysis, the interactions between TiCp2 and S8/Li2Sn (where n = 1, 2, 4, 6, and 8) were investigated.
· Typically, the heterogeneous catalyst on the cathode surface, for example, the basal plane of transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) or single-atom catalyst (SAC)-based materials, are found to interact with the LiPSs in two binding configurations.52 The Li binds with nonmetal atoms, whereas transition metal atoms on t…
· In transition metal-based SACs, polysulfides adsorbed through the TM-S bond (where S is from the LiPSs) regulate the LiPS adsorption behavior and electrocatalysis.
· Herein, to find the most favorable binding configuration for the TiCp2 homogeneous catalyst, we initially positioned the LiPS species in the vicinity of both the cyclopentadienyl rings and the Ti atom of TiCp2.

· To understand the efficacy of the studied homogeneous catalyst in capturing the LiPS at homogeneous interfaces and suppressing the shuttle effect, we studied the binding energies of TiCp2-LiPS with those of the DME-LiPS systems.
· Although the actual electrolyte system is quite complex, consisting of lithium salts, solvents, and additives,57 the key components involved in the shuttle phenomenon are the solvent molecules; hence, we considered only electrolyte solvents for our study.
· Notably, LiPSs interact more extensively with DME molecules than they do with DOL in a commonly used mixed electrolyte solution of DME and DOL.58-60 This suggests that LiPS would largely associate with the DME in the electrolyte environment.

· We also calculated the binding energies of LiPSs with TiCp2 using the COSMO- SMD implicit solvent model.
· Overall, the binding energies of the higher-order LiPSs with TiCp2 are higher than those of electrolyte solvents when explicit solvents are considered.
· In addition, the weightage of vdW interaction was observed to decrease with increased lithiation in contrast to the trend of vdW binding energies, attaining the lowest value in the case of Li2S for both gas and solvent phases (R about 25-30%).
· To understand the discharge performance of LiSBs with homogeneous catalysts, we investigated the entire SRR process on TiCp2.
· The Gibbs free energy calculation details for SRR are provided in our previous study.63 Long-chain soluble LiPSs, namely, Li2S8, Li2S6, and Li2S4, are formed as a result of the reaction between Li ions and S8 species, which in turn finally convert to insoluble Li2S2 and Li2S, due to the accelerated reduction process wi…
· Figure 6 shows the free energies of each SRR step on the TiCp2 catalyst in both gas and solution phases.
· For comparison, we calculated the SRR free energies for the isolated LiPSs both in the vacuum and also in the solvent phase.
· The results indicate spontaneous exothermic reactions for the first step of reduction of S8 to Li2S8 (ΔG = -6.153 eV (gas phase) and -6.147 eV (solvent phase)) on TiCp2.
· The reduction of Li2S8 to Li2S6 is also exothermic for both the gas (ΔG = -0.591 eV) and the solvent phase (ΔG = -0.494 eV).

· Typically, the fourth and fifth reduction steps involving the transformation of Li2S4 to Li2S2 and Li2S are considered as the rate-limiting steps in the overall discharge process of LiSBs.64 The statement is well corroborated with the observed higher values of ΔG for isolated Li2S2/Li2S formation with ΔG = 1.240 eV and…
· We found that the TiCp2 catalyst greatly reduces the Gibbs free energies of the rate-limiting step of Li2S4 →Li2S2 to ΔG = 0.972 eV (gas) and ΔG = 0.759 eV (solvent).
· Again, for the conversion of Li2S2 → Li2S, the barriers are reduced to ΔG = 0.719 eV (gas) and ΔG = 0.591 eV (solvent).
· That is, when compared with the rate- determining SRR of isolated LiPS in the vacuum, the energy barrier with the aid of the TiCp2 catalyst is reduced by 21.6%.
· Our simulation results clearly demonstrate that the energy barrier for the rate- determining step in the presence of the TiCp2 catalyst is smaller than that without TiCp2, illustrating the faster rate of conversion of higher-order LiPSs into short-chain Li2S because of a lower reaction energy barrier, thus strongly pro…
· The proposed reaction mechanism of LiSB on the TiCp2 homogeneous catalyst is illustrated in Figure 7.
· The chosen catalyst, TiCp2, dissolves in the electrolyte as an additive.
· Hence, TiCp2 on the electrolyte solution tends to bind with Li2S8 to form the TiCp2-Li2S8 complex, liberating the DME.
· This observation illustrates that the studied TiCp2 catalyst could effectively accelerate the reduction of soluble higher-order polysulfides to insoluble Li2S.
· It is important to note that as the homogeneous catalyst undergoes molecular interactions with polysulfides, it can increase the utilization of the catalyst; that is, the addition of a lower amount of TiCp2 into the electrolyte could accelerate a larger amount of LiPS.33 Thus, the Li2S nucleation and growth process is …
· Hence, the addition of TiCp2 into the electrolyte reduces the LiPS shuttling due to their stronger chemical interaction and accelerates the SRR kinetics at homogeneous interfaces, which is essential for the practical realization of LiSBs. 4.
· CONCLUSIONS In summary, for the first time, the homogeneous catalyst, TiCp2, is introduced into the commonly used electrolyte as an additive for Li-S batteries.
· We used DFT calculations to understand the potential of TiCp2 as a homogeneous catalyst by systematically exploring their interaction with LiPSs at homogeneous interfaces.
· The electrochemical stability window calculations reveal that TiCp2 can work as an additive to accelerate the reduction kinetics of soluble LiPSs because of the electrochemical inactivity within the operating potential range of LiSBs.
· We studied the binding behavior of TiCp2 with various LiPSs in both gas and solvent phases.
· Further, the comparison of calculated binding energies with that of the commonly used DME electrolyte solvent demonstrates the effectiveness of the studied homogeneous catalyst in suppressing the shuttling of polysulfides.
· Proposed TiCp2 homogeneous catalyst-boosted sulfur redox cycle mechanism for Li-S batteries.
· Furthermore, the Gibbs free energy profiles of SRR steps reveal that all of the intermediate reactions are more favorable, and barriers are much lower in the presence of the TiCp2 catalyst compared with isolated LiPS in vacuum and solvent phases.
· Moreover, the homogeneous catalyst-promoted catalytic conversion of S8 to Li2S is an indication of improved Li2S deposition on the cathode with a decrease in LiPS accumulation in the electrolyte, which favorably improves the capacity retention and electrochemical reaction kinetics.
· Overall, this study provides an improved understanding of the homogeneous catalytic process in LiSBs using titanocene as a representative additive to electrolytes.

#### P382 | 10.1002/adma.202411197 | Unveiling the Reaction Mystery Between Lithium Polysulfides and Lithium Metal Anode in Lithium-Sulfur Batteries
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=N 因素=多硫化物与锂金属反应机理、LiNO3 抑制作用（以浸泡为主） Ⅱ=N 备注=存疑：Li-S 电池性能比较是否成立待核
相关句 111（正文）+ 93（SI），已截断
· Herein, the reaction rate and products of LiPSs with Li metal anode, the composition and structure of the as-generated solid electrolyte interphase (SEI), and the mechanism of lithium nitrate (LiNO3) additives for inhibiting the corrosion reactions are systematically unveiled.
· Concretely, LiPSs react with Li metal anode more rapidly than Li salt and generate a Li2S-rich SEI.
· LiNO3 functions dominantly by modulating the solvation structure of LiPSs and inherently reducing the reactivity of LiPSs, rather than the conventional understanding of LiNO3 participating in the formation of SEI.
· Mater. 2024, 36, 2411197 © 2024 Wiley-VCH GmbH 2411197 (1 of 10) www.advancedsciencenews.com www.advmat.de concentration of LiPSs in anolyte, adopting composite Li metal or Li alloy to improve the structural stability, and employing ad- ditives to generate a protective SEI, etc.[13] Among them, lithium nitrate (LiNO3) …
· Moreover, the mechanism of the LiNO3 ad- ditive in inhibiting the side reaction remains controversial.
· Con- sidering the inevitably dissolved LiPSs in electrolytes that react violently with Li metal anode, answering the above fundamental questions is expected to greatly clarify the actual reaction mech- anism of Li metal anode in Li-S batteries and innovate rational Li metal anode protection strategies in high-energy-de…
· Herein, we systemically unveil the reaction rate and products of LiPSs and Li metal, clarify the composition, structure, and properties of the as-generated SEI, and elucidate the mechanism of the LiNO3 additive on side reaction inhibition.
· Compared with Li salt, the LiPSs react more rapidly with Li metal and yield a Li2S-rich SEI on the surface of Li metal anode.
· Furthermore, the LiNO3 addi- tive is found to function dominantly by participating in the sol- vation structure of LiPSs and thereby reducing the intrinsic reac- tivity of LiPS on Li metal, rather than mainly participating in the formation of SEI and substantially altering the SEI composition.
· The as-proposed mechanism of LiNO3 on inhibiting the shuttle eﬀect indicates solvation structure modulation are more eﬀective strategies for Li metal anode protection in Li-S batteries. 2.
· Li2S8 was se- lected as a representative LiPS to study the side reaction with Li metal as Li2S8 is generally considered with higher reactivity and contributes dominantly to the LiPS shuttle eﬀect.[15] Lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfony)imide (LiTFSI) as a commonly used Li salt was also evaluated as its reaction with L…
· To decouple the origin of the complex S-containing SEI, we sequentially investigated the reaction products of Li metal with electrolytes containing Li2S8 or LiTFSI.
· The commonly used 1,3-dioxolane (DOL) and 1,2- dimethoxyethane (DME) with a volume ratio of 1:1 were used as the basic solvent.
· The Li2S8 electrolyte was prepared by dissolving Li2S8 in DOL/DME with a total sulfur concentration of 1.0 M, and the LiTFSI electrolyte was 1.0 M LiTFSI dissolved in DOL/DME.
· The reaction of Li metal with each electrolyte was studied by analyzing the cycled Li metal anodes in Li | Li symmetric cells.
· As shown in Figure 1a(1), only high-valence sulfur species are detected in the S 2p XPS spectrum of the SEI generated in LiTFSI electrolyte, which comes from the decomposition of LiTFSI itself (Figures S1 and S2, Supporting Information).
· In contrast, only low-valence sulfur species especially Li2S is detected in the S 2p XPS spectrum of the SEI generated in Li2S8 electrolyte (Figure 1a(2)).
· Moreover, the sulfur content is significantly higher than the SEI generated in LiTFSI electrolyte (10.0% at surface and 12.6% at 60 s sputtering time, Figure S8 and Table S1, Supporting Information).
· The competition between Li2S8 and LiTFSI in the reaction with Li metal was further investigated by dissolving Li2S8 in LiTFSI electrolyte with a total sulfur concentration of 1.0 M (donated as Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte) considering both Li2S8 and LiTFSI exist in working Li-S batteries.
· As shown in Figure 1a(3), the SEI generated in Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte exhibits characteristics dominantly aﬀorded by Li2S8 with only low-valence sulfur species including Li2S, Li2S2, and incompletely reacted LiPSs in the S 2p XPS spectrum.
· It is worth mentioning that the above results diﬀer from the observations regarding a large amount of high-valence sulfur species on the surface of Li metal anode reported in many Li- S battery studies.[17] Considering that there are no species capa- ble of oxidizing LiPSs or Li2S in the Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte, the r…

· It should be noted that the content of the F element in- creases abnormally even compared with the SEI generated in LiTFSI electrolyte (Figures S17,S18, and Table S1, Supporting Information).

· The atomic mass ratio of S/F in SEI significantly increases with Li2S8 addition in the electrolyte (Figure 2e), again indicating the reaction between LiPSs and Li metal is dominant.
· The reaction results between LiPSs and Li metal were also observed by im- mersing a piece of Li metal in an electrolyte and aging it for 24 h.
· The aged Li metal in LiTFSI electrolyte maintains its orig- inal flat morphology (Figure 2f), while many pits with a diame- ter exceeding 10 µm appear on the surface of the aged Li metal in Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte (Figure 2g; Figure S29, Supporting Information).
· To assess the performance of the Li2S-rich SEI formed in LiPS-containing electrolytes, a series of model experiments following Figure 3a were conducted.

· In contrast, the morphology of the Li plated in Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte is uneven with many dendritic Li in di- ameter of less than 1 µm (Figure 3c; Figure S31b, Supporting In- formation).

· One of the most widely used methods to suppress the LiPS shuttle eﬀect is adopting the LiNO3 additive.[15] For understanding the work- ing mechanism of this LiNO3 additive, previous works suggest LiNO3 mainly functions by participating in the formation of SEI.
· For instance, Zhang et al. pointed out that LiNO3 can oxide LiPSs to high-valence sulfur species while LiNO3 is reduced to LiNO2 to construct a compact and stable SEI.[22] However, this proposed mechanism is a bit rough, and increasing the N-O species in SEI is not always eﬀective in prolonging the cycling lifespan of …
· Therefore, clarifying the working mechanism of LiNO3 on inhibiting the LiPS shuttle eﬀect is highly important for ratio- nally stabilizing Li metal anodes in Li-S batteries.
· The suppressing of LiPS corrosion was first verified by im- mersing a bare Li foil in the electrolyte composed of 1.0 M[S] Li2S8 dissolved in 1.0 M LiTFSI in DOL/DME with 2 wt.% LiNO3 addi- tive (donated as Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte).
· However, the diameter of the corrosion pits is ≈2 µm, which is significantly lower than the Li metal aged in Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte.
· The inhibition of LiPSs shuttle eﬀect by LiNO3 was further quantified.
· See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License www.advancedsciencenews.com www.advmat.de electrolyte or Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte (Figure S36, Sup- porting Information)…
· After the addition of LiNO3, the shuttle current is significantly reduced from 0.090 to 0.010 mA.
· The im- pact of the suppression of LiPS corrosion on Li metal by LiNO3 was further clarified by using Li | Ni cells.
· After the addition of LiNO3, the Coulombic eﬃciency of the Li | Ni cell at the 1st cycle increases from 65.5% to 89.1% (Figure S37a, Support- ing Information).
· Moreover, after 15 cycles, the Coulombic eﬃ- ciency of the Li | Ni cells with the Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte be- gins to decrease rapidly, while the Coulombic eﬃciency of the Li | Ni cells with the Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte still remains at more than 97% (Figure S37b, Supporting Information).
· The above results comprehensively confirm that the LiNO3 additive eﬀectively alleviates the corrosion reaction between LiPSs and Li metal.
· To gain insights into the LiNO3 working mechanism, we fur- ther investigated the eﬀect of LiNO3 on the reaction products of the electrolyte containing LiPSs and Li metal.
· The Li metal cycled in Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte was analyzed by XPS and ToF-SIMS.
· Moreover, the atomic concentration of sulfur in the SEI is significantly lower than that in the SEI generated in the electrolyte without LiNO3 (Figures S39,S40, and Table S1, Supporting Information).
· The above results indicate that the addition of LiNO3 can hardly oxidize the reaction products of LiPSs and Li metal, while the reaction degree between LiPSs and Li metal is indeed reduced.
· Notably, the content of nitrates including NO3 - and NO2 -derived from LiNO3 is rather low in N 1s XPS spec- trum as well (Figure S42, Supporting Information), indicating LiNO3 hardly reacts with Li metal to generate massive nitrogen- containing components in SEI.
· Furthermore, the concentration of LiNO3 was increased to 5 wt.% in the Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte to verify the above conclusions (donated as Li2S8-LiTFSI-5LiNO3 electrolyte).
· Under the same Li metal cycling conditions, the S 2p XPS spectrum of the Li plated in Li2S8-LiTFSI-5LiNO3 electrolyte still shows only low-valence sulfur without high-valence sulfur signals (Figure S43, Supporting Information).
· The atomic con- centrations of F and N are also lower than that of S, indicating that LiTFSI and LiNO3 contribute little to the reaction with Li metal (Figures S44 and S45, Supporting Information).
· The above re- sults imply that LiNO3 neither oxidizes the disadvantageous low- valence sulfur species into high-valence states nor contributes ni- trogen species to the SEI to a significant extent.
· The electrolyte was 1.0 M LiTFSI and 2 wt.% LiNO3 in DOL/DME (1:1, by vol.) (donated as LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte) with an elec- trolyte to sulfur ratio (E/S ratio) of 7.0 µL mgS -1.
· In summary, LiNO3 neither changes the sulfur species in SEI nor significantly participates in the forma- tion of SEI regarding the reaction between LiPS electrolyte and Li metal.
· The 3D reconstruction of the SEI by ToF-SIMS exhibits similar results as the SEI derived from Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte (Figure S49, Supporting Information).
· However, it takes a longer sputter- ing time of 1155 s to reach the maximum intensity of the S-sig- nal in the SEI derived from the Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte, again indicating the reaction between LiPSs and Li metal is inhib- ited (Figure 4c; Figure S50, Supporting Information).
· To sum up, the addition of LiNO3 reduces the sulfur content and suppresses the reaction between LiPSs and Li metal, but the key SEI charac- teristics regarding sulfur, fluorine, and nitrogen species change little.
· Bulk Li2S still exists in the SEI generated in Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte in addition to Li2O, while LiF and Li3N can hardly be identified possibly due to their low content in SEI (Figure 4d; Figure S51a, Supporting Information).
· The SEI composition is consistent with the results of the SEI derived in Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte without LiNO3.
· The only diﬀerence in SEI morphology is that the addi- tion of LiNO3 thickens the SEI to 80-100 nm (Figure 4e; Figure S51b, Supporting Information).
· Also, the content of S and F ele- ments decreases along the SEI outward in comparison with the SEI generated without LiNO3 (Figure 4e1-e2).
· Based on the above results, the addition of LiNO3 indeed in- hibits the side reaction between LiPSs and Li metal but no sig- nificant change is observed in the as-generated SEI, especially the sulfur and nitrogen species.
· How LiNO3 eﬀectively inhibits the shuttle eﬀect requires further exploration.
· Concretely, does the SEI really inhibit the reaction of LiPSs with Li metal de- spite no obvious change in composition, or is the intrinsic re- activity of LiPSs and Li metal eﬀectively suppressed by LiNO3 through LiPS solvation structure modulation without significant SEI change?
· To answer the above questions, we supposed 3 pos- sible mechanisms of how LiNO3 inhibits the LiPS shuttle eﬀect (Figure 5a). 1) The addition of LiNO3 alone generates a highly stable SEI against LiPS corrosion; 2) The synergistic eﬀect of LiNO3 and LiPSs forms a highly stable SEI against LiPS cor- rosion; 3) LiNO3 parti…
· To verify the first 2 hypotheses, the spe- cific SEI was prepared by immersing a piece of bare Li in an electrolyte containing LiNO3 (donated as LiNO3 treated Li) or both LiPSs and LiNO3 (donated as Li2S8-LiNO3 treated Li).
· Af- terward, the Li metal with the as-generated SEI was transferred to electrolyte with Li2S8 and LiTFSI but without LiNO3 to quan- titatively measure the consumption rate of Li2S8.

· The hydrogen, lithium, carbon, nitrogen, oxygen, fluorine, and sulfur elements are represented by white, purple, grey, blue, red, cyan, and yellow, respectively. e) 7Li NMR spectra of Li2S8-LiTFSI or Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte. f) LUMO energy level comparison of S82-in Li2S8 and Li2S8-NO3-. in Li2S8-LiTFSI electrol…
· The specific SEI generated in the electrolyte containing LiNO3 or both LiPSs and LiNO3 was first clarified.
· The S 2p XPS spectrum of the LiNO3 treated Li mainly contains high-valence sulfur species derived from LiTFSI reduction and only a small amount of Li2S exists (Figure S52a, Supporting Information).
· The SEI on Li2S8-LiNO3 treated Li aﬀords only low-valence sulfur species in the S 2p XPS spectrum without LiTFSI-related signals (Figure S52b, Supporting Information).
· After 24 h, ≈18.8% Li2S8 is consumed by LiNO3 treated Li, which is slightly lower than the Li2S8 consumption rate of bare Li (Figure 5b; Figure S53, Supporting Information).
· Meanwhile, 30.7% Li2S8 is consumed by Li2S8-LiNO3 treated Li, demonstrating the introduction of LiNO3 in the existence of LiPSs cannot improve the stability of the as-generated SEI against LiPS corrosion (Figure S54, Supporting Information).
· The Li2S8 consumption rate of bare Li in LiNO3-containing electrolyte was then mea- sured.
· Concretely, 0.04 wt.% LiNO3 was added in the electrolyte composed of 20 mM[S] Li2S8 and 20 mM LiTFSI in DOL/DME.
· Combining the above results, we conclude that the LiNO3 additive does not function mainly by producing a stable SEI in LiPS-containing electrolyte, and the inhibited LiPS shuttle eﬀect is mainly caused by the reduced LiPS intrinsic reactivity through LiNO3 participating in the LiPS solvation structure.
· The participation of LiNO3 in the LiPS solvation structure was further verified both theoretically and experimentally.
· The sys- tems of 1.0 M[S] Li2S8 and 1.0 M LiTFSI in DOL/DME with or without 2 wt.% LiNO3 were investigated by molecular dynamics (MD) simulations.
· As shown in Figures S56 and S57 (Support- ing Information), in the system of Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte, Li+ appears at the closest position of 2.1 Å from the center of S8 2-.
· The solvation power of DOL is too weak to participate in the LiPS solvation structure within 6 Å.[13b] In the system of Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte, the appearance positions of the above species do not change significantly, but NO3 -appears at only 3.0 Å away from the S8 2-center, which is even closer than DME (Figu…
· NMR was further employed to verify the solvation structure change of LiPSs by LiNO3 addition.
· LiPSs can form ion-ion interactions with Li salts and ion-dipole interactions with solvents through Li+ fol- lowing the Li bond theory.[24] Therefore, the solvation structure of LiPSs can be probed by monitoring the chemical environ- ment of Li+.
· After the addition of LiNO3, the chemical shift of 7Li moves downfield from -1.45 to -1.16 ppm (Figure 5e).[25] This result demonstrates that LiNO3 reduces the electron cloud density of Li+ solvated by S8 2-, which implies that LiNO3 indeed interacts with LiPSs in electrolytes.
· Furthermore, the eﬀect of the interaction between LiNO3 and LiPSs is probed by density func- tional theory (DFT) calculations.
· The energy level of the lowest unoccupied molecular orbital (LUMO) of Li2S8 and Li2S8-LiNO3 cluster were evaluated.
· The LUMO of Li2S8 is not changed af- ter interacting with LiNO3 (Figure S59, Supporting Information).
· However, the LUMO energy level of S8 2-in Li2S8-LiNO3 is in- creased from -0.81 eV in bare Li2S8 to -0.49 eV, indicating that the interaction of LiNO3 improves the reduction stability of Li2S8 (Figure 5f).
· The above experimental and simulation results to- gether disclose that LiNO3 participates in the solvation structure of LiPSs and increases the reduction stability of LiPSs intrinsi- cally.
· Contrary to the conventional understanding, the addition of LiNO3 neither changes the sulfur species in SEI nor participates in the formation of SEI itself to a significant extent.
· Model ex- periments and theoretical simulations indicate the mechanism of LiNO3 in inhibiting the LiPS shuttle eﬀect is modulating the solvation structure of LiPSs to reduce the inherent reactivity in- stead of violently changing the SEI.
· Nitrates with other cations or organic functional groups may aﬀord higher protection capability than LiNO3.
· Additives with Lewis basic sites may also aﬀord suitable interaction with LiPSs to better inhibit the corro- sion reaction or improve the protection durability.
· Keywords corrosion, lithium metal anode, lithium polysulfides, lithium-sulfur bat- teries, solid electrolyte interphase Received: July 30, 2024 Published online: August 16, 2024 [1] S.
· ［SI］Preparation of electrolyte The LiTFSI electrolyte was composed of 1.0 M LiTFSI in DOL/DME (1:1, by vol.).
· ［SI］The Li2S8 electrolyte was prepared by mixing Li2S and S8 with a molar ratio of 8:7 and a total sulfur concentration of 1.0 M in DOL/DME.
· ［SI］The Li2S8-LiTFSI electrolyte was prepared by mixing Li2S and S8 with a molar ratio of 8:7 and a total sulfur concentration of 1.0 M in LiTFSI electrolyte.
· ［SI］The LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte was composed of 1.0 M LiTFSI with 2 wt.% LiNO3 in DOL/DME.
· ［SI］The Li2S8-LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte was prepared by mixing Li2S and S8 with a molar ratio of 8:7 and a total sulfur concentration of 1.0 M in LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte.
· ［SI］The Li2S8-LiTFSI-5LiNO3 electrolyte was prepared by mixing Li2S and S8 with a molar ratio of 8:7 and a total sulfur concentration of 1.0 M in LiTFSI electrolyte with 5 wt.% LiNO3.
· ［SI］The above electrolytes were prepared in an Ar-filled glove box with H2O and O2 contents below 0.01 ppm. 1.3.
· ［SI］SEI composition characterization For analyzing the Li metal in Li | Li symmetric cells used for SEI composition characterization, the Li | Li cells were assembled with Li metal foils as both the cathode and anode with a diameter of 15.0 mm, and the amount of electrolyte was 40 µL.
· ［SI］Each cell was cycled 5 times under a current density of 1.0 mA cm-2 and an areal capacity of 1.0 mAh cm-2 to ensure continuous reaction between Li metal and electrolyte.
· ［SI］For analyzing the Li metal anode in Li-S cells used for SEI composition characterization, the Li-S cells were assembled with sulfur cathodes with a diameter of 13.0 mm, Li metal foils as the anode with a diameter of 15.0 mm, PP membranes as the separator, and LiTFSI-LiNO3 electrolyte as the electrolyte.
· ［SI］The electrolyte to sulfur ratio (E/S ratio) was 7.0 µL mgS-1.
