#### P383 | 10.1002/advs.202404245 | Impact of Glyme Ether Chain Length on the Interphasial Stability of Lithium-Electrode in High-Capacity Lithium-Metal Battery
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=C 因素=甘醇二甲醚链长 G2 / G3 / G4（LiTFSI 或 LiNO3 + LiBr） Ⅱ=N 备注=存疑：是否有锂硫电池比较待核
相关句 97（正文）+ 25（SI）
· In this regard, the Li-deposition stability largely depends on the electrolyte, which reacts with Li to form a solid electrolyte interphase (SEI) with diverse physico-chemical properties, and dictates the interphasial kinetics.
· Therefore, optimizing the electrolyte for stability and performance remains pivotal.
· Hereof, glyme ethers are an emerging class of electrolytes, showing improved compatibility with metallic Li and enhanced stability in Li─Air and Li─Sulfur batteries.
· Yet, the criteria for selecting glyme solvents, particularly concerning Li deposition and dissolution processes, remain unclear.
· The SEI characteristics and Li deposition/dissolution processes are investigated in glyme-ether-based electrolytes with varying chain lengths, using lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI) and lithium nitrate (LiNO3) salts under high capacity and limited electrolyte conditions.
· Longer glymes led to more homogeneous SEI, particularly pronounced with LiNO3, minimizing surface roughness during stripping, and promoting compact Li deposits.
· This study clarifies factors guiding the selection of glyme ether-based electrolytes in Li metal batteries, oﬀering insights for next-generation energy storage systems.
· In this study, we undertook a comprehensive investigation to systematically explore the characteristics of the SEI, interphasial kinetics, and the morphologies of Li deposition/dissolution in glyme-ether-based electrolytes with varying chain lengths, and their impact on the cycle-life of the cell.
· We specifically inves- tigated electrolytes containing a Li+ concentration of 1 m with two widely utilized Li salts in Li─Air and Li─S batteries: either lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI) or lithium nitrate (LiNO3).
· To meet the practical requirements of high en- ergy density cells, our experimental design involved cell cycling under conditions of high areal-capacity of 4 mAh cm-2, lim- ited electrolyte content of 5-6 g Ah-1, and a current density of 0.2 mA cm-2, unless otherwise mentioned.
· Our research outcomes unveiled noteworthy trends: longer chain-length gly- mes demonstrated superior cycle life, regardless of the Li salt used.
· However, our comparison of Li salts indicated that LiTFSI exhibited inferior performance compared to LiNO3 with any glyme used.
· These observed outcomes are attributed to the dif- ferences in the solvation structure depending on the glyme chain length and the electrolyte salt, which influence the de- position/dissolution kinetics, and the propensity for electrolyte decomposition, resulting in varied SEI properties.
· Overall, this research elucidates critical parameters influencing glyme ether- based electrolytes in LMBs, providing valuable insights for ad- vancing the next-generation energy storage systems. 2.

· These are the widely used electrolytes in Li─Air and Li─S batteries, where the Li metal negative electrode is required to endure cycling at high capacity.[42-46] The incorporation of LiBr in the electrolytes is a well-established strategy aimed at reducing the high charge over- potential in Li─Air batteries and facilit…
· Figure 1a illustrates the voltage profiles corresponding to the first-cycle Li stripping and plating processes of the Li|Cu cells, employing 1.0 m LiTFSI + 0.05 m LiBr electrolytes in G2, G3, and G4 solvents (referred to as TB-G2, TB-G3, and TB-G4, respectively).
· Figure 1b presents similar voltage profiles for 1.0 m LiNO3 + 0.05 m LiBr electrolytes with G2, G3, and G4 solvents (denoted as NB-G2, NB-G3, and NB-G4, respectively).
· Interest- ingly, within these LiNO3-based electrolytes, the first-cycle CE re- mained within the range of ≈75% to 95%.
· A comparative analy- sis of the voltage profiles revealed two notable distinctions when employing diﬀerent glymes with LiTFSI and LiNO3 salts.
· First, LiTFSI and shorter glyme electrolytes exhibited reduced volt- age polarization attributed to their higher ionic conductivities, as shown in Figure S2 (Supporting Information).
· Second, LiTFSI- based electrolytes exhibited lower CE.
· In fact, repeated experiments revealed significant variability in the first-cycle CE for each glyme, especially in LiTFSI electrolytes.
· Figure 1c,d present the number of cycles for the LiTFSI and LiNO3-based electrolytes, respectively.
· Notably, as depicted in the voltage versus time curves in Figures S3 and S4 (Supporting In- formation), all cells experienced failures due to short-circuits, with more pronounced occurrences observed in LiTFSI-based electrolytes.
· As observed in Figure 1c,d, the cycling stability trends for both LiTFSI and LiNO3-based electrolytes follow the same order: G2 < G3 < G4.
· Besides, these findings unequivo- cally establish that cycling stability is superior in LiNO3-based electrolytes compared to LiTFSI-based electrolytes, irrespective of the glyme solvent employed.
· Based on these results, we then compared the cycling stabilities of Li-O2 cells using NB-G2, NB- G3, and NB-G4 electrolytes.
· Figure 2b shows the mor- phologies of the Li electrodes after 4 mAh cm-2 stripping in var- ious electrolytes.
· Stripping in LiTFSI-based electrolytes resulted in notable surface inhomogeneity spanning several micrometers to tens of micrometers on the Li metal surface.
· Galvanostatic voltage profiles for Li stripping/plating in Li|Cu cells at 0.2 mA cm-2 current density using a) LiTFSI and b) LiNO3-based electrolytes. c,d) Cycling stability of the same cells. extent of physical damage to the electrode surface was consider- ably lower in LiNO3-based electrolytes.
· The observed morphologies of Li are no- tably influenced by the choice of Li salt and can be categorized into two distinct types.
· LiTFSI promotes a mixed morphology of moss and whisker-like deposits, whereas spherical morpholo- gies are formed in LiNO3-based electrolytes.
· In LiNO3-based electrolytes, glyme-dependent morphological dif- ferences are also observed in the deposited Li.
· Upon closer exami- nation of Figure 2c, variations in the dimensions of the deposited Li are evident, depending on the glyme solvent used with LiNO3.
· Interestingly, a considerable variation in Li morphologies is observed in this scenario too, which cannot be readily attributed solely to a specific type of Li salt.
· These diﬀerences in morphological trends of deposited Li on Cu and previously stripped Li surfaces underscore the significant influence of not only the physical properties of electrolytes but also the inter- phasial properties of the electrode on deposition morphologies.
· Nevertheless, it is essential to recognize that the properties and composition of the electrolyte are crucial in determining the interphasial properties of the electrode.
· Therefore, it is imperative to achieve a comprehensive understanding of how electrolyte composition influences interphasial kinetics, SEI formation, and the deposition process to establish correlations between electrolyte type, Li deposition morphology, and cell performance.
· Interpretations of the Electrolyte-Dependent Morphologies 2.3.1.
· Stripping from Li Our investigation initially focuses on exploring the variations in Li electrode morphologies during stripping, which are influ- enced by the electrolyte composition.
· When a current is applied, Li+ cations migrate through the SEI layer and dissolve in the electrolyte.
· Figure 3a-f depicts the quantitative analysis of Li compounds within the surface layer after the strip- ping of Li electrodes under varying electrolyte conditions.
· Results in Figure 3a-c reveal that after stripping in LiTFSI-based electrolytes with any glyme, the surface layer exhibits significant chemical heterogeneity, with no clear trend dependent on the glyme type.
· Although these data do not establish a clear trend, it is apparent that the use of the longest glyme, G4, yields enhanced chemical homogeneity when paired with LiNO3 as the electrolyte salt and the ratio of Li2O to Li2CO3 decreases with decreasing glyme chain length in the order, NB-G4 > NB-G3 > NB-G2.
· To directly observe the compositional inhomogeneities on the Li electrode surface, we performed energy dispersive X-ray (EDX) mapping after stripping in NB-G2, NB-G3, and NB-G4 electrolytes and the results are shown in Figures S20-S22 (Supporting Informa- tion).
· Figure 4a,b depict the Nyquist plots derived from the elec- trochemical impedance spectra (EIS) of Li|Li symmetric cells immersed in LiTFSI and LiNO3-based electrolytes, respectively.
· Comparing Figure 4c,e, it is evident that the interphasial impedances (RSEI) in LiTFSI-based electrolytes are significantly higher than those in LiNO3.
· This discrepancy may be attributed to the increased decomposition of LiTFSI-based electrolytes and the accumulation of more insulating compounds on the electrode surface, leading to higher RSEI values.
· Inter- estingly, Figure 4d,f shows a clear trend of decreasing charge transfer resistance (RCT) as the glyme chain length decreases for both LiTFSI and LiNO3.
· Nyquist plots of Li|Li symmetric cells using a) LiTFSI-based and b) LiNO3-based electrolytes.
· Interphasial resistance (RSEI) for c) LiTFSI and e) LiNO3-based electrolytes.
· Charge-transfer resistance (RCT) for d) LiTFSI and f) LiNO3-based electrolytes. 2.3.2.
· Plating on Cu The deposition of Li on the Cu electrode reveals a dependency on both the Li salt and the glyme solvent.
· Specifically, longer glymes and LiNO3 as the electrolyte salt inhibit dendritic/whisker-like Li deposition.
· Despite the initially flat surface of the Cu electrode, significant variations in deposition morphologies underscore the influence of electrolyte properties.
· The trends observed in Figure 4d,f indicate slower charge transfer kinetics with increas- ing glyme chain length and the use of LiNO3 as the electrolyte salt.
· Instead, we measured the electronic conductivity distribution on the Cu electrode using peak force tunneling atomic force microscope (PFTUNA) after a galvanostatic sweep of Li|Cu cells in diﬀerent electrolytes from the open circuit voltage (OCV) to 0 V versus Li/Li+.
· The color scale for the conductivity represents the relative electronic conductivity values on the surface. electrolyte-dependent surface film is formed.[55] Due to the diﬀerences in reduction propensities, unique characteristics are expected in surface films formed by diﬀerent electrolytes.
· However, after the galvanostatic sweep, an increase in inhomogeneity is observed in any electrolyte, except for NB-G4.
· In LiTFSI-based electrolytes (Figure 6b-d), the inhomogeneity is more pronounced, which further intensifies as the glyme size decreases.
· XPS results in Figure 3 indicate that the electrolyte decomposition products in TB-G2, TB-G3, and TB-G4 are primarily Li2O and Li2CO3, which are insulators with wide bandgaps.[56,57] Consequently, these insulating compounds are also expected to accumulate on the Cu surface, causing spatial variations in electronic cond…
· In contrast, the relatively uniform current distributions with NB-G3 and NB-G4 can be attributed to limited electrolyte decomposition after the voltage sweep up to 0 V versus Li/Li+.
· Therefore, due to the low reductive stabilities, the accumulation of insulating compounds resulting in highly inhomogeneous electronic conductivity distribution in LiTFSI-based electrolytes, especially in TB-G2 and TB-G3, leads to sporadic nucleation and growth, and spatially variable Li morphologies.
· Conversely, the more uniform surface (Figure 6e-g) and relatively slower kinet- ics in LiNO3-based electrolytes promote spherical Li deposition across the electrode surface.
· In situ optical micrographs of Li deposition on Cu wire electrodes at selected time intervals in (a) TB-G4, (b) TB-G3, (c) TB-G2, (d) NB-G4, (e) NB-G3, and (f) NB-G2. in diﬀerent electrolytes were directly observed in situ using an optical microscope.
· The images clearly depict the uneven nucleation and growth of mossy and whisker-like Li structures in LiTFSI- based electrolytes.
· Moreover, the diﬀerences in glyme length-dependent stability of the electrolyte with the deposited Li is to some extent evident through the in situ micro- scopic experiments.
· The Li deposited in the TB-G4 electrolyte exhibits a distinct metallic luster.
· In contrast, the TB-G2 and TB- G3 electrolytes result in a dark grey deposit.
· Conversely, in LiNO3-based electrolytes, the nucleation and growth of Li appear more homogeneous and compact, irrespective of the glyme solvent used. 2.3.3.
· However, using G2 as the solvent in NB-G2 leads to both whisker-like and spherical Li morphologies due to enhanced inhomogeneity in the interphase and higher kinetics.
· In reality, the solvents and anions present in the double layer also undergo reduction, re- sulting in the formation of the SEI.
· The reductive stability of the electrolyte dictates the SEI properties, and the spontaneous de- composition of highly unstable electrolytes leads to a thick SEI composed of insulating Li compounds with spatial heterogene- ity.
· When electrons are transferred from the electrode surface to solvated species, the transferred electron can reside on the Li+ center, the attached solvents/anions, or the exterior of the sol- vation sphere.[58] Previous studies have shown that this electron distribution depends significantly on the solvation structure …
· Density func- tional theory (DFT) calculations suggest that in cases of smaller glymes, the transferred electron is distributed within the ionic radius of the metal center and on the organic part of the sol- vation sphere.[58] This indicates competition between metal ion reduction and solvent decomposition, with electr…
· With shorter glyme lengths, solvent reduc- tion propensity increases, and electrode reaction kinetics acceler- ate, primarily limited by the electron transfer step.
· Apparently, this enhanced stability of the electrolyte due to higher solvation in longer glymes seems contradictory to the large num- ber of reports favoring solvents with weaker solvation strength to exclude the solvents from the primary solvation shells and induce contact ion pairs or aggregated structures with the a…
· In contrast, we compare the relative stabilities of diﬀerent glyme solvents under the completely solvated conditions of Li+ ions at a relatively lower salt concentration of 1 m, where the solvents are expected to be predominantly present in the primary sol- vation shell.
· See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License www.advancedsciencenews.com www.advancedscience.com to chelation provide better stability to the electrolyte.
· Current measurements using PFTUNA on the reduced Cu electrode surface revealed, in Figure 6, higher inhomogeneity with smaller glyme electrolytes due to the accumulation of insulating elec- trolyte decomposition products.
· Furthermore, higher charge transfer kinetics (Figure 4d,f) in smaller glyme electrolytes exacerbate the inhomogeneous stripping/plating.
· However, similar to previous reports, when employing LiNO3 as the electrolyte salt, its highly associated nature results in the retention of NO3 -ions in the primary sol- vation shell as contact ion pairs, and these ions predominantly occupy the inner Helmholtz plane (IHP) on the Li electrode.[64] Consequently, LiNO3-b…
· Furthermore, LiNO3 reduces charge transfer kinetics compared to LiTFSI, as observed in Figure 4d compared to Figure 4f.
· The combined eﬀects of these phenomena promote stable Li stripping/plating in LiNO3-based electrolytes.
· Therefore, the diﬀerences in sol- vation structure based on glyme chain length and electrolyte salt are considered the underlying factors contributing to the diﬀerences in electrolyte stability, interphasial properties, and deposition/dissolution kinetics.
· Conclusion In summary, we have explored the impact of glyme chain length on the stability of Li metal electrode and the cycle life of high areal-capacity LMB cells with limited electrolyte volume.
· To gain insights into the morphological changes during Li stripping and plating, we investigated the physicochemical properties of the SEI and the interphasial kinetics of the stripping/plating reac- tion across diﬀerent electrolytes.
· Our electrolyte compositions included three glymes with varying chain lengths - diglyme, triglyme, and tetraglyme - combined with 1 m LiTFSI or LiNO3 salts and 0.05 m LiBr as an additive.
· We observed that reducing the glyme chain length increased both interphasial kinetics and SEI inhomogeneity, particularly pronounced with LiTFSI as the electrolyte salt.
· Conversely, longer glymes (G3 and G4) with LiNO3 salt exhibited more uniform stripping and compact spherical Li deposition.
· These variations in interphasial prop- erties, Li deposition morphologies, and cycle life are attributed to diﬀerences in solvation structure dependent on glyme chain length and electrolyte salt.
· According to previous density func- tional theory (DFT) calculations, smaller glymes with weak cation solvation undergo competition between metal ion reduction and solvent decomposition, leading to electron transfer with facile ki- netics via an outer sphere pathway without breaking the primary solvation sheath.
· Conversely, stronger solvation with longer gly- mes like G4 does not allow the electron to enter the primary sol- vation shell instantaneously and requires a partial desolvation to facilitate metal ion reduction, slowing down the kinetics and also minimizing electron transfer to associated solvents.
· Additionally, LiNO3-based electrolytes retain NO3 -ions in the primary sol- vation shell as contact ion pairs, primarily occupying the inner Helmholtz plane (IHP) on the Li electrode, thereby reducing sol- vent decomposition feasibility.
· The slower kinetics and favorable SEI properties promote stable Li stripping/plating in longer gly- mes and LiNO3-based electrolytes.

· The in-situ dissolution of PEO changes the localized solution environment around C/S particles and suspends the diffusion of polysulfides (LiPSs), while the large number of mesopores retained inside C/S particles can ensure the good infiltration of electrolyte and act as a container for LiPSs.
· Currently, the mainstream Li-S cells use an ether electrolyte with DOL and DME as binary solvents.
· Based on the “solid-liquid-solid” reaction processes in such electrolyte system, LiPSs will continuously migrate between the positive and negative electrodes and a series of complex side reactions will occur, nominating, the shuttle effect [13,14].
· Typically, the formation of the shuttle effect involves the following five steps: (1) formation of long-chain polysulfides, (2) detachment of pol ysulfides from the cathode conducting surface, (3) dissolution of poly sulfides into the electrolyte, (4) migration of polysulfides to the lithium anode side, and (5) reactio…
· From a different angle, reducing E/S [29] or opti mizing electrolyte solvent [30,31] are effective ways to reduce the amount of LiPSs dissolved.
· Compared with conventional coin cells, in the pursuit of high energy density pouch cells, with the increase of sulfur load, N/P decrease, and E/S decrease, most strategies mentioned above that work well in coin cells cannot be directly extended to pouch or the suppression of shuttle effect in pouch cells is no longer a…
· When the electrolyte infiltrates the cathode, the dissolved PEO provides a PEO-rich electrolyte environment around the C/S, and the resistance of LiPSs to migrate away from the cathode to the anode is greatly increased due to the reduced mobility caused by the increased viscosity of the local electrolyte environment an…
· In addition, since PEO is distributed only on the outside of the C/S composite, the large number of mesopores retained inside the C/S composite still remains, ensuring that the entire interior of the C/S composite is well wetted by the electrolyte.
· Although ~20 µm particle size is much larger than the commercial Li-ion materials (1~2.5 µm for typical LFP cathode), it is crucial for Li-S cathode to maintain enough pores and accommodate electrolyte for the conversion type battery Fig. 1.
· Here, to confirm the proposed design, the cathode with PEO@C/S was soaked in DME/DOL solvent, after which the mesopores was released again.
· It should be noted that the washed cathode shows slightly higher pore number than the cathode without PEO, suggesting that the dissolution of polymer can reopen the channels, thus providing more space for electrolyte infiltration.
· The introduction of PEO fills the gaps within due to prolonged stirring, making it hard for electrolyte to infiltrate. 2.2.
· To visualize the process, we made a polymer film and immersed it into the solvent, demonstrating the fast dissolution of PEO when con tacting the electrolyte (Fig.
· To mimic the localized solvation envi ronment after dissolution, we conducted the viscosity and ion conductivity tests using electrolytes with and without 2 wt% PEO.
· As shown in Table S2, the viscosity of the W/O PEO electrolyte is only 1.62 cP with the ion conductivity of 1.108 mS cm-1, while that of the PEO dissolved electrolyte is 25.9 cP with the slightly lower ion conductivity of 0.969 mS cm-1.
· To gain a deeper understanding of the influence of introducting PEO towards the solvation and migration of LiPSs in the electrolyte, Raman spectroscopy was used to characterize different LiPSs containing electrolytes.

#### P435 | 10.1021/acs.jpclett.4c02789 | In-Operando FTIR Spectroscopy Study on Rapid Polysulfide Binding via Caffeine in Lithium-Sulfur Batteries

· Lett. 2024, 15, 12164-12170 Read Online ACCESS Metrics & More Article Recommendations * sı Supporting Information ABSTRACT: Lithium-sulfur batteries are limited by the high mobility of polysulfides in the electrolyte, which allows them to migrate from the cathode to the lithium anode.
· Various cathode additives have been shown empirically to mitigate this problem, although the mechanism is not often ascertained experimentally.
· Herein, we demonstrate for the first time that the small molecule additive caffeine reduces the polysulfide shuttling current in a lithium-sulfur battery and decreases the capacity fade in galvanostatic cycling experiments.
· Of the few studies leveraging this technique for lithium-sulfur batteries, most have investigated polysulfide speciation.22-25 Some works also investigated electrolyte decomposition.26,27 Beyond these, in-operando FTIR studies of LSBs in real time are limited.
· Our study demonstrates the utility of this technique beyond speciation to tackle the mechanistic understanding of polysulfide interaction with the molecular additive caffeine.
· Motivated by the natural material approach and the use of polar functional groups to bind polysulfides, we studied caffeine as a cathode additive in LSBs.
· To first study the effect of caffeine in a LSB, it was incorporated as a solid powder additive into the cathode (Figure S1, SEM images), with the carbonyl groups of caffeine remaining unchanged during the fabrication process, as seen by FTIR and X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS) measure- ments (Figures S2 and S3).
· The caffeine additive also mitigated this effect.
· Furthermore, there is no signal from the solvent in this region that masks the carbonyl peaks.
· Prior to the onset of cycling, the liquid electrolyte can solvate a majority of the caffeine additive.
· However, as cycling commences, the liquid electrolyte begins to deplete due to SEI formation.44,45 SEI reactions that persist throughout cycling may lower the amount of caffeine that can remain solvated, and thus we see the emergence of a solid and likely microcrystalline phase of caffeine.
· Furthermore, it has been found that, after the initial discharge, lithium polysulfides remain in the electrolyte, even when the battery is charged.41 This could lead to a third species of caffeine that is likely due to an interaction with these remaining polysulfides.
· In this work, caffeine was studied as a novel cathode additive for Li-S batteries.
· Shuttle current measurements showed that the incorporation of caffeine into the cathode reduced the polysulfide concentrations in the electrolyte.
· Research progress on gel polymer electrolytes for lithium-sulfur batteries.

· In this study, we apply in operando sulfur X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) to a model system to elucidate the LiNO3 protection mechanism.
· The electrolyte is designed to be sulfur- free such that sulfur XAS can faithfully track the evolution of polysulfide and their derived species during charge-discharge.
· By probing the electrolyte on both the cathode side and anode side, we can obtain a more accurate understanding of the reactivity of LiNO3 and its role in stabilizing Li-S batteries.
· The commonly used electrolyte formulation for Li-S batteries is lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTF- SI) dissolved in 1,3-dioxolane (DOL) and 1,2-dimethoxy- ethane (DME) with LiNO3 as the additive.
· LiNO3 is understood to react with polysulfide (PS) to form products that stabilize the battery from polysulfide shuttling.
· However, precisely determining the LiNO3 reactivity is nontrivial because the salt and PS both contain sulfur.
· To simplify the electrolyte formulation and to accurately determine the working mechanism of the LiNO3 additive, we formulated an electrolyte using LiNO3 as the only salt dissolved in DOL and DME in 1:1 volume ratio (Figure 1a).
· Hence, any sulfur species found in the electrolyte must be from the dissolved polysulfide or its derived products.
· The concentration of LiNO3 chosen for detailed study is 0.5 M because this concentration is widely studied in the literature.20-22 In terms of weight percentage, it is equivalent to around 3.5 wt % in a 1 M LiTFSI in DOL/DME electrolyte.
· The measured ionic conductivity (Figure S1) and Coulombic efficiency (Figure S2) are typical for LiNO3-containing electrolytes.
· Switching from LiTFSI to LiNO3 does not change the fundamental electro- chemical behavior which is the conversion reaction from S8 to Li2S during discharge and vice versa during charge (Figure S3).23 The charge-discharge profile is not influenced by the concentration of LiNO3 either (Figure S4).
· Long-term cycling of Li-S batteries assembled with the 0.5 M LiNO3 dissolved in DOL/DME electrolyte deliver good electrochemical perform- ance, maintaining more than 80% of initial capacity even after 100 cycles (Figure 1b).

· A common trend of PS oxidation across the electrolyte indicates that the reaction leading to their formation is unaffected by the applied voltage and is more chemical in nature.
· Like in any chemical redox reaction, the oxidation of PS requires an oxidizer, which has to be NO3 -, the only available oxidizer in the electrolyte.
· Nitrogen K- edge spectra support this point, showing NO2 -species being observed along with the NO3 -in the cathode-electrolyte interphase (Figure 3a), electrolyte (Figure 3b), and the anode- electrolyte interphase (Figure 3c).25 NO2 -is the reduced product formed because of the chemical oxidation of PS by NO3 -.
· The source of PS in the electrolyte is the cathode, which produces PS during cycling.
· The high solubility of PS in ether-based electrolytes allows PS to leave the cathode and enter the electrolyte.
· PS then diffuses across the electrolyte and reaches near the Li metal anode where it can react to form Li2S on the Li metal interphase.
· Since the diffusion is happening from the cathode side, the electrolyte near the cathode should always have a higher concentration of PS unless the concentration is homogenized across the cell.
· However, the continued operation of the battery will prevent homogenization since the cycling of the sulfur cathode will always produce more PS that can dissolve in the electrolyte.

· Herein, we report a pre-potentiostatic strategy to induce in situ reductive decomposition of LiPF6 based carbonate electrolytes, with the formation of a compact and LiF-rich cathode electrolyte interphase (CEI) on a S8@S7Se/PAN composite cathode with a high sulfur loading content.

· Notably, the formation of soluble lithium polysulfide (LiPS) intermediates in ether- based electrolytes leads to their diffusion driven redistribution, trig gering the well-known “shuttle effect” [3].
· This phenomenon results in electrolyte contamination, corrosion of the lithium anode, and rapid capacity and energy density degradation-effects that are particularly detrimental when the total cell mass is considered.
· Moreover, the multiphase nature of this redox pathway necessitates a large number of electrode-electrolyte interfacial contact sites, requiring high electrode porosity (50-60 %) to enable efficient reaction kinetics [4].
· This requirement is inherently at odds with the need for densely packed electrodes featuring high sulfur loading (>5 mg cm-2) [5], thereby exacerbating electrolyte consumption and diminishing practical energy density.

· This study presents FePC@NH2-MIL-68, a biomimetic enzyme catalyst stable in ether- based electrolytes, to address these issues, which can replace LiNO3 and facilitate Li-anion decomposition.
· Consequently, the FePC@NH2-MIL-68 cell, without LiNO3 additive, delivers a high discharge capacity of 1549 mAh g-1 at 0.2 C and a low capacity decay rate of 0.067% over 1000 cycles at 1.5 C.

· This work emphasizes that in developing sulfur conversion catalysts, special attention must be paid to their effects on other lithium salt components, which provides a guidance for new catalysts design for metal-sulfur batteries. 1.
· To address these challenges, Mikhaylik and Akridge first proposed lithium nitrate (LiNO3) as an electrolyte additive in Li-S batteries in 2008 [20].
· The primary mechanism behind this improvement involves LiNO3 promot- ing the formation of a stable solid electrolyte interface (SEI) layer be- tween the lithium anode and the electrolyte [21,22].
· This SEI layer enhances the stability of the lithium anode, prevents further electrolyte decomposition, reduces self-discharge, inhibits the lithium polysulfide (LiPS) shuttle effect, and thereby improves Coulombic efficiency [23, 24].
· Meanwhile, LiNO3 can also create a protective LixNOy layer on the * Corresponding authors.
· All rights are reserved, including those for text and data mining, AI training, and similar technologies. cathode, known as the cathode-electrolyte interphase (CEI) [27].
· This layer could potentially control the decomposition of lithium salts and prevent the reduction of LiPS on the cathode [28].
· However, LiNO3 limits the electrochemical stability window of the electrolyte [29], with deeper discharges causing more irreversible nitrate reduction [30] and adversely affecting the redox reversibility of the sulfur cathode [31].
· Most importantly, in the future commercialization process of Li-S bat- teries, large pouch cells or cylindrical cells require a significant amount of electrolyte, leading to a substantial amount of LiNO3 being added [32].
· If the internal temperature of the battery runaway, the carbon/- sulfur composite materials present in the cathode can chemically react with LiNO3, potentially forming hazardous gunpowder that significantly increases the risk of explosions [33].
· Unfortunately, few studies have directly replaced LiNO3 while forming stable CEI/SEI [35].
· Catalysts, such as black phosphorus quantum dots, show promise as alternatives, which were found to catalyze LiPS conversion and main- tained CE >98% when LiNO3 was omitted from the battery electrolyte [37].
· However, most reports on “LiNO3 mimic” are merely focused on forming a stable CEI/SEI or accelerating the conversion of LiPSs, but neglect the interaction between LiNO3 and LiTFSI.
· Interestingly, LiNO3, LiTFSI and LiPS are all Li salts in the Li-S system and the design of LiPS conversion catalysts offers a new approach to replace LiNO3 for LiTFSI decomposition.
· Recent research has shown that loading enzymes or organometallic small molecules into MOFs can mimic enzyme active centers to achieve biomimetic catalysis [40].
· Combining these findings with our previous works, we believe that MOF-based biomimetic enzyme catalysts have excellent potential for promoting the decomposition of both LiTFSI and LiPSs.
· Herein, we have successfully encapsulated iron(II) phthalocyanine (FePC) within amino-functionalized MIL-68(In) MOF to construct an FePC@NH2-MIL-68 catalyst for advanced Li-S batteries.
· Through a comprehensive series of in-situ and ex-situ spectroscopic analyses, coupled with ab initio molecular dynamics simulations, we discovered that FePC@NH2-MIL-68 can effectively capture LiTFSI via In-N bonds, and replace LiNO3 to promote LiTFSI decomposition, forming stable SEI/CEI layers.
· Besides, this novel catalyst can also accelerate the con- versions of lithium polysulfides and significantly reduce the reaction energy barrier (Fig. 1a).

· Specifically, the stick-shaped FePC@NH2-MIL-68 can be conveniently obtained by self-assembly of indium salt, 2-aminoterephthalic acid (NH2-H2BDC) and FePC (more detailed synthetic procedure please see the experimental section in Supporting Information).
· The microstructure and composition of this catalyst were analyzed by X-ray Diffraction (XRD), Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy (FT-IR), Raman spectroscopy, scanning electron microscopy (SEM), and transmission electron microscopy (TEM).

#### P567 | 10.1021/jacsau.5c00537 | Transformation of Polysulfide Catholyte Chemistry through Lithium-Arene Complexes for Superior Solubility and Cyclability in Li-S Batteries
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=A 因素=溶液正极合成路线：Li+联苯+S vs Li2S+S Ⅱ=N 备注=存疑：是否算电解液配方比较待核
相关句 99（正文）+ 14（SI）
· Transformation of Polysulfide Catholyte Chemistry through Lithium- Arene Complexes for Superior Solubility and Cyclability in Li-S Batteries Ngoc Long Le, Sih-Ling Hsu, Thi Hang Vu, Chi-You Liu, Quang Huy Dinh, Avi Arya, Elise Yu-Tzu Li,* and Yu-Sheng Su* Cite This: JACS Au 2025, 5, 3866-3878 Read Online ACCESS Metrics…
· We report an innovative catholyte synthesis method using lithium-arene complexes, offering significant advancements in terms of solubility, stability, and scalability.
· By leveraging the interaction of metallic lithium with biphenyl (BP) and sulfur, we developed a Li+BP+S catholyte formulation that outperforms conventional Li2S +S systems.
· The Li+BP+S catholyte demonstrates superior solubility, achieving up to 12 M active sulfur and faster dissolution rates at lower temperatures, reducing preparation times by 66%.
· Electrochemical evaluations revealed enhanced capacity retention, with the catholyte maintaining 83.2% of its initial capacity after 500 cycles and exhibiting minimal capacity fading of 0.03% per cycle.
· The Li+BP+S system also demonstrated high-rate capability and long-term stability, retaining significant capacity under lean electrolyte conditions.
· The concept of a “catholyte”, where LiPSs are predissolved in the electrolyte, has been proposed to improve cathode fabrication.
· Fu et al. introduced a liquid catholyte using LiPSs integrated with a free-standing current collector, achieving high initial charge capacities at varying rates.14 The conventional synthesis of LiPSs typically involves the codissolution of Li2S and sulfur in an electrolyte.
· To address these issues, this study introduces a novel synthesis approach using the reaction of metallic lithium with arenes and sulfur to produce LiPS catholytes.
· This method, illustrated in Figure 1, achieves high solubility and rapid preparation of LiPS catholytes by leveraging lithium- arene complexes.
· The resulting catholyte offers superior capacity and enhanced cycling stability and eliminates the need for toxic Li2S, thereby supporting safer large-scale production and improved LSB performance.
· By leveraging this innovative synthesis technique, we aim to revolutionize the production of LiPS catholytes, paving the way for more efficient, scalable, and sustainable LSB technologies. 2.
· Synthesis of Polysulfide Catholytes The electrolyte was prepared by dissolving 1.85 M lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide salt (LiTFSI; Sigma-Aldrich) and 0.1 M lithium nitrate additive (LiNO3; Kanto Chemical) in a 1:1 (v/v) mixture of 1,3-dioxolane (DOL; Thermo Scientific) and 1,2-dimethoxyethane (DME; Alfa-Aes…
· A traditional Li2S+S polysulfide solution was synthesized by mixing Li2S powder (Alfa- Aesar) and sublimed sulfur (Showa Chemical) in a 1:5 molar ratio with the prepared electrolyte solution, as reported previously.14 The mixture was stirred and heated under controlled conditions until all of the solutes were completel…
· The Li+BP+S polysulfide catholyte was prepared by chemically reacting metallic lithium, biphenyl (Thermo Scientific), and sublimed sulfur in a 1:1:3 molar ratio in the prepared electrolyte solution.
· First, the concentrated polysulfide catholyte was added onto a free-standing carbon nanotube (CNT; Taiwan Carbon Materials Corp.) electrode prepared using a previously reported CNT papermaking process.17 A Celgard 2400 separator was placed over the catholyte-absorbed CNT electrode, and the electrolyte solution was unif…
· Symmetric cells were assembled using two identical CNT electrodes, each loaded with 20 µL of 1 M polysulfide solution and 20 µL of 1 M electrolyte, separated by a Celgard separator.
· Materials Characterization Ultraviolet-visible spectroscopy (UV-Vis; Jasco V-370) was employed to analyze the LiPS species in the catholyte solutions. 7Li NMR analysis of the catholyte was performed by using a JEOL ECZ500R/S1 NMR500 spectrometer.
· Schematic illustration of the dissolution mechanisms for (top) the conventional catholyte synthesis method using lithium sulfide and sulfur (Li2S+S) and (bottom) the developed method utilizing metallic lithium, biphenyl, and sulfur (Li+BP+S).
· The new approach achieves enhanced solubility, accelerates preparation, and eliminates the need for toxic and air-sensitive Li2S, enabling the safer and more scalable production of LiPS catholytes.
· To evaluate the solubility limits of Li+BP+S and Li2S+S polysulfide solutions, we conducted experiments to dissolve each type of polysulfide in different electrolytes with varying lithium salt concentrations.
· In an electrolyte solvent containing 1.85 M LiTFSI salt, Li+BP+S polysulfide achieves a maximum solubility of 4 M active sulfur, whereas Li2S+S polysulfide reaches only 1.5 M (Figure 2a).
· When the LiTFSI salt concentration is reduced to 1 and 0 M, the polysulfide solubility increases significantly.
· Notably, the solubility of Li +BP+S remains consistently higher than that of Li2S+S, achieving values of 8.5 and 12 M compared with 6.5 and 10 M for Li2S+S in 1 and 0 M electrolytes, respectively (Figures 2b and S2).
· This difference highlights the role of BP in facilitating faster and more efficient dissolution at lower temperatures, making the Li+BP+S system a more practical option for catholyte preparation.
· Additionally, higher BP concentrations (e.g., 1Li + 2BP + 3S) increase the maximum solubility of active sulfur to 6 M in a 1.85 M electrolyte (Figure S5).
· Maximum solubility of polysulfide solutions in (a) 1.85 and (b) 1 M electrolytes.
· (d) Maximum solubility of polysulfide in electrolytes with varying LiTFSI salt concentrations.
· In summary, the optimized preparation condition for a concentrated Li+BP+S polysulfide solution involves the use of a molar ratio of 1Li+2BP+3S, achieving up to 6 M sulfur solubility in a 1.85 M electrolyte, with complete dissolution attained at 80 °C.
· This condition offers a favorable balance between solubility, processing time, and temperature, making it optimal for catholyte preparation in practical applications.
· Material Characterization of Polysulfide Catholytes UV-vis spectroscopy was used to evaluate the dissolved species of the Li+BP+S and Li2S+S polysulfide systems.
· These findings suggest that the conversion mechanism of Li+BP+S polysulfides may differ from that of traditional Li2S+S formulations during discharge in a lean electrolyte LSB setup.

· Electrochemical Evaluation of Polysulfide Catholytes Galvanostatic charge-discharge (GCD) tests were conducted to assess the capacity of cells containing different polysulfide catholytes.
· Figure 4a,b present the first, 50th, 200th, and 500th cycle charge-discharge profiles at 0.5C for LSBs with Li+BP+S and Li2S+S catholytes.
· A detailed analysis of the discharge curves for the Li+BP+S and Li2S+S catholytes is shown in Figure 4c,d, respectively.
· Both catholytes demon- strate two distinct discharge stages: the first plateau near 2.25 V corresponds to the conversion of sulfur into LiPSs (Li2Sn, where n ranges from 4 to 8), whereas the second plateau at approximately 2.05 V is associated with the formation of insoluble Li2S2 and Li2S.13,33 For the Li+BP+S catholy…
· In contrast, the Li2S+S catholyte has a lower initial discharge capacity of 343.9 mAh g-1 (82% of the theoretical value) and decays to 247.4 mAh g-1 after 500 cycles.
· The cycling performance at 0.5C for the Li+BP+S and Li2S +S catholytes is illustrated in Figure 4e, with the electrolyte-to- sulfur (E/S) ratio fixed at 31.25 µL mgS -1.
· Both catholytes display good electrochemical stability when paired with a CNT current collector.14,34 The Li+BP+S catholyte achieves an initial discharge capacity of 1115.5 mAh g-1 and maintains 927.8 mAh g-1 after 500 cycles, with minimal capacity fading of 0.03% per cycle and nearly 100% Coulombic efficiency.
· In comparison, the Li2S+S catholyte has an initial capacity of only 925.1 mAh g-1, which decreases to 713.3 mAh g-1 after 500 cycles, corresponding to a reduction in degradation rate of 0.046% per cycle, indicating inferior cycling stability.
· To evaluate the impact of catholyte formulation modifications, biphenyl was replaced with naphthalene to form a Li+Naph+S catholyte.
· Additionally, the long-term stability of the Li+BP+S polysulfide solutions was assessed by assembling cells up to 30 days after catholyte preparation (Figure S7).
· The Li+BP+S catholyte retained a high capacity and exhibited excellent stability, highlighting its durability.
· To demonstrate the practical viability of the Li+BP +S catholyte for high-energy-density LSBs, cells were assembled with low E/S ratios.35,36 The corresponding sulfur areal loadings were 1.45 mg cm-2 for E/S = 10.42 µL of mgS -1 and 2.17 mg cm-2 for E/S = 7.8 µL of mgS -1, allowing evaluation under practically relevant…
· After 200 cycles, these capacities stabilize at 544.8 and 579.2 mAh g-1, with the cell at an E/S ratio of 7.8 µL mgS -1 retaining over 100% capacity, showcasing outstanding stability with reduced electrolyte usage.
· Rate performance tests for both catholytes, conducted across varying current densities (0.1-4 C and back to 0.1C), are displayed in Figure 4g.
· The Li+BP+S catholyte demonstrates superior rate capability, achieving a capacity of 742.3 mAh g-1 at 4C and recovering to 1,391.1 mAh g-1 when the current is returned to 0.1C, highlighting its efficient sulfur utilization and excellent electrochemical reversibility.
· In contrast, the Li2S+S catholyte exhibited a lower capacity of 631.2 mAh g-1 at 4C, reflecting a limited rate performance.
· To provide a broader perspective on the performance of our catholyte formulation, a benchmarking summary has been included in Table S1, which compares the Li+BP+S system with those of other reported polysulfide catholytes.
· Our system shows comparable or even better capacity retention and cycling stability, even under low E/S ratios, and does so using a simple CNT paper current collector without any functional catalysts or engineered interlayers.
· Given that few new polysulfide catholyte formulations have been reported in recent years, the performance of the Li+BP+S system highlights its strong potential to advance LSB development.
· Figure 5a shows the initial CV curves of the two catholytes, revealing two cathodic peaks at ∼2.2 and ∼2.0 V.
· These peaks correspond to the transformation of sulfur into higher-order LiPSs (Li2Sn, n ≥4) and their subsequent reduction to Li2S2 and Li2S.36,37 For the Li+BP+S catholyte, a slightly lower reaction voltage of 1.91 V is observed for solid-phase sulfur conversion.
· Figure 5b,5c depict the CV curves over the first four cycles for the Li+BP+S and Li2S+S catholytes, respectively.
· The higher peak currents in the Li+BP+S catholyte suggest enhanced active sulfur utilization and additional capacity contributions.
· Symmetrical cell CV tests (Figure 5d-f) revealed stronger and broader peaks in the Li+BP+S catholyte, indicative of more complete redox reactions and efficient catalytic transformation of LiPSs.36 EIS data for the two catholytes at different cycles are shown in Figure 5g-i.
· Initially, the charge transfer resistance (Rct) of the Li+BP+S catholyte (137 Ω) is slightly greater than that of the Li2S+S catholyte (121 Ω) (Figure 5j),36,38 likely because the presence of BP increases the charge transfer resistance.
· However, the Rct decrease is more pronounced for the Li+BP+S catholyte, reflecting its uniform distribution of active sulfur species within the electrode.
· With extended cycling, the Li+BP+S catholyte shows a slight increase in electrolyte resistance (Re) due to higher ion concentrations and viscosity,36,38 resulting from greater LiPS dissolution.
· Additionally, the interfacial resistance (Rint), influenced by the deposition of solid sulfur/lithium sulfide films,23 is higher for the Li+BP+S catholyte because of its greater active sulfur utilization. 3.4.
· Polysulfide Catholyte Postcycling Analysis To examine the morphology and surface structure of the electrodes, SEM imaging was conducted.

· Oxidized sulfur species, such as sulfite ([SO3]2-) and lithium thiosulfate ([S-SO3]2-), are observed at ∼165.7- 167.2 eV, likely arising from LiTFSI and LiNO3 reduction products.43 The broad peak at ∼168.7-170.4 eV corresponds to sulfate ([SO4]2-) species, which are likely derived from side reactions during cycling.
· Simulation of Arene-Aided Lithium Dissolution To further elucidate the role of arenes in lithium dissolution and polysulfide formation, we performed density functional theory (DFT) simulations to investigate the interactions among lithium, solvent molecules, and arene additives.
· We consider the ability of single Li and double Li to bind to solvent components, arenes, or DME.
· On the other hand, the DME solvent molecule cannot effectively stabilize the Li atom since both are electron-donating (Figure S12).
· Therefore, Li metal cannot dissolve directly in the DME solvent in the absence of sulfur, but the dissolution may be greatly enhanced by arene molecules, as observed in the experiments.
· We then consider the competition of the adsorption of soluble LiPSs (Li2S8, Li2S6, and Li2S4) on arenes or DME solvent molecules.
· These improvements stem from the role of BP in facilitating polysulfide formation, accelerating sulfur ring breakdown, and leading to more efficient catholyte processing.
· Electrochemical evaluations confirmed that the Li+BP+S catholyte enables increased sulfur utilization and long-term cycling stability.
· At an E/S ratio of 31.25 µL mgS -1, the system retains 927.8 mAh g-1 after 500 cycles, with a minimal capacity fade (0.03% per cycle).
· Even under lean electrolyte conditions (E/S = 7.8 µL mgS -1), it maintains over 100% capacity after 200 cycles, demonstrating excellent electrolyte utilization and reversibility.
· Comprehensive material characterization (UV-Vis, 7Li NMR, Raman, and XRD) revealed that BP effectively inhibits LiPS clustering, enhances Li2+ transport, and promotes uniform sulfur distribution on the CNT electrode, contributing to stable catholyte-electrode interfaces.
· Theoretical modeling further indicated that BP stabilizes lithium dissolution without disrupting polysulfide conversion mechanisms, reinforcing its critical role in catholyte design.
· In summary, the Li+BP+S system presents a scalable and high-performance catholyte formulation that enhances sol- ubility, processability, and electrochemical performance, making it a promising candidate for next-generation high- energy-density LSBs.
· Future work will focus on optimizing the catholyte composition to accelerate redox kinetics and minimize parasitic reactions, as well as developing engineered cathode architectures to maximize sulfur utilization and power density under lean electrolyte conditions.

· Article | Article in Press https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-77258-w Unlocking High-Valent Chalcogen Redox with Halide-Rich Electrolytes for High-Energy Lithium Batteries Yinlin Shen1, Hao Zhang1, Yuxuan Wu1, Mingzi Sun 1, Doudou Feng1, Jiaqian Qin 2, Zhengyu Shi 1, Bolong Huang 1 & Jijian Xu 1,3 Elemental chalcogens …
· Here, we report a halide-rich electrolyte design that enables reversible high-valent chalcogen redox in lithium batteries.
· By using soluble organic halide salts with asymmetric cation, the electrolyte provides active chloride or bromide anions to promote high-valent redox and stabilize oxidized inter- mediates, thereby enabling a redox-amphoteric selenium (Se) conversion pathway.
· Challenges such as polyselenide dissolution and shuttle, slow heterogeneous phase evolution, and unstable electrode/ electrolyte interphases collectively prevent even this two-electron limiting from being fully realized6,7.
· In these electrolytes, Cl-(or ClO4 -) acts as the key charge-compensating anion that activates chalcogen oxidation and stabilizes the resulting oxidized products.
· Analogously, in Al15-17 and Mg batteries18, ionic- liquid and organic electrolytes are used to avoid hydrolysis and, criti- cally, the strongly Lewis acidic chloroaluminate species (e.g., Al2Cl7 -, AlCl4 -) promote oxidation of chalcogens to higher valent states (S0/S+, Se0/Se+, Te0/Te4+).
· Recent advances, such as the 3.6 V anode-free sodium-sulfur (Na | |S) battery utilizing thionyl chloride (SOCl2)-based electrolyte, have demonstrated the potential of high-valent S0/S4+ (S/ SCl4) chemistry19.
· These limitations highlight an urgent need for a rational electrolyte design that provides high, mobile halide activity to activate and stabilize the high-valent Se0/Se+ redox couple.
· Here, we address this challenge by designing a nonaqueous lithium electrolyte incorporating organic halide salts with asymmetric cation architectures (Tributylammonium chloride, TBMACl; Tributylmethylammonium bromide, TBMABr).
· This electrolyte design delivers high halide solubility and effective halide activity, unlocking a reversible high-valent Se0/Se+ conversion in a nonaqueous lithium system, featuring a distinct ~2.6 V plateau.
· Importantly, this electrolyte concept generalizes to activate high-valent reactions in Li | |S and Li | |SeS2, delivering pronounced gains in discharge capacity.
· On the other hand, Se also exhibits a comparatively low ionization energy (first ionization energy, 940.9 kJ mol-1), implying that oxidation to high-valent cationic selenium species (Se+, Se2+, Se4+) is accessible if the electrolyte can effectively activate and stabilize the oxidized states.
· When an organic electrolyte simultaneously supplies electrochemically active Li+ and coordinating halide anions (Cl-/Br-), Se can therefore be driven into a redox-amphoteric conversion mani- fold: during discharge, Se is reduced by two electrons and couples with Li+ to form Li2Se; during charge, Se is oxidized to high-…

· The Designed-Cl electrolyte exhibits good compatibility with Se2Cl2, the charged product formed during high-valent Se redox (Supplementary Fig. 2).

· The electrochemical activation of high-valent states was investi- gated via a charge-first protocol, which provides direct evidence for electrolyte-dependent accessibility of the oxidative Se0/Se+ reaction.
· When charged from the initial state (Supplementary Fig. 8), the Li | |Se cell in Baseline electrolyte exhibits no capacity.
· In stark contrast, the cell in Designed-Cl electrolyte delivers a clear charge capacity of 180 mAh g-1, demonstrating that the Se0/Se+ oxidation pathway is uniquely activated by the high chloride activity of the designed elec- trolyte.
· Upon the subsequent first discharge, the Li | |Se cells in the Baseline and Designed-Cl electrolytes delivered specific capacities of 591 and 1050 mAh g-1, respectively, corresponding to gravimetric specific energies of 1218 and 2003 Wh kgSe -1 based on Se mass, or 584.6 and 961.4 Wh kgelectrode -1 based on the total p…
· The advantage persists upon cycling: in the 2nd/3rd cycles, Li | |Se cell in Baseline electrolyte delivers discharge capacities of 503/482 mAh g-1, whereas Li | |Se cell in Designed-Cl electrolyte maintains capacities of 980/958 mAh g-1, indicating mark- edly improved reversibility and stability in the halide-containin…
· The 2nd-cycle voltage profile in Designed-Cl electrolyte (Fig. 1c) exhibits high Coulombic efficiency, consistent with a highly reversible three-electron conversion process.
· These distinct electro- chemical signatures corroborate the successful activation of a three- electron Se2-/Se0/Se+ reaction manifold, fundamentally enabled by the halide-anion chemistry of the designed electrolyte.
· Upon further charging, the Se-Se band under- goes a discernible red shift from 233 to 231 cm-1, and an additional band emerges at 326 cm-1, which is characteristic of Se-Cl bonding and consistent with the reference Raman spectrum of Se2Cl2 dissolved in the Designed-Cl electrolyte (Supplementary Fig. 13).
· The concomitant appearance of the Se-Cl mode and the Se-Se red shift thus provides direct spectroscopic evidence for electrolyte-enabled formation of Se2Cl2 at the positive electrode upon charging.

· Collectively, these results indicate that introducing Cl-into the electrolyte not only modulates the solvation structure but also improves the overall reaction kinetics of the Li | |Se system.
· Design of non-aqueous electrolyte The oxidation of elemental Se is kinetically demanding.
· Consequently, accessing the Se0/Se+ reaction requires an electrolyte that provides high-activity, coordinatively competent charge-compensating anions within a sufficiently wide stability window.
· Activating the high-valent I0/ I+ redox chemistry of I2 by introducing Cl⁻ or Br-into the electrolyte has been widely reported in aqueous Zn batteries27-31, translating this strategy to non-aqueous media remains a challenge.
· We address this by using organic halide salts, whose cation structures tune ion pairing and dissociation (Supplementary Fig. 16).
· Theoretical calculations of reaction pathways for the Se oxi- dation/reduction process in (g) chlorine- and (h) bromine-containing electrolytes.
· Solvent selection is equally critical for achieving both high salt solubility and controlled intermediate transport32-34.
· Compared with ether solvents (DME, Diglyme (DEGDME), DOL), DMI has a high dielectric constant (ε = 37.6), which promotes dissolution of organic halide salts and ion separation, while its moderate viscosity can sup- press dissolution and long-range diffusion of oxidized products (Se2Cl2) and lithium polyselenide interme…
· Solubility mea- surements of a series of organic chloride and bromide salts in DME and DMI (Supplementary Fig. 18) confirm this design rationale: owing to their low dissociation energies combined with the strong solvating capability of DMI, TBMACl and TBMABr reach solubilities exceeding 3 M in DMI, providing a practica…
· Raman spectroscopy (Fig. 4e, f and Supplementary Figs. 19-21) provides a direct probe into the local coordination environment of DMI solvent.
· By contrast, adding 1 M TBMACl to DMI produces only a modest shift of the C = O band to 1692 cm-1, indicative of comparatively weak TBMA+-solvent interac- tions.
· Notably, the Raman spectrum of TBMACl/DMI exhibits no additional discernible bands beyond those of DMI in the probed region, suggesting that the organic halide salt does not introduce prominent additional vibrational features and that its primary role is to modulate ionic speciation rather than solvent structure.
· In the fully formulated Designed-Cl elec- trolyte, the DMI carbonyl band shifts further to a higher wavenumber, indicating a stronger perturbation of the C = O group. 1H NMR (Fig. 4g and Supplementary Figs. 22, 23) provides com- plementary evidence for Li+-solvent coordination.
· The upfield shifts become more pronounced in the higher- salt-concentration Designed-Cl and Designed-Br electrolytes, con- sistent with strengthened Li+-DMI interactions.
· In addition, clear halogen signals are detected in the 35Cl NMR spectrum (Fig. 4h) of the Designed-Cl electrolyte and in the 81Br NMR spectrum (Supplementary Fig. 23) of the Designed-Br electrolyte, supporting the presence of highly available halide species and thus high halide activity in these electrolytes.
· Classical molecular dynamics (MD) simulations on the Designed- Cl electrolyte were conducted with the resulting data shown in Fig. 4i-k and Supplementary Data 1.

· The extensive participation of anions in the Li+ solvation sheath suggests an anion-rich solvation sheath, which is expected to facilitate interfacial Li+ desolvation by weakening purely solvent-dominated coordination.
· The extracted tracer diffusion coefficients (also termed self-diffusion coefficients) from the MSD of individual ionic species are 5.36 × 10-6 cm2 s-1 for Li+ and 6.15 × 10-6 cm2 s-1 for Cl-, revealing relatively fast dynamics in the designed electrolyte.
· The tracer diffusion coefficients are calculated to quantitatively reveal the intrinsic dynamic behavior of Li+ and Cl-in the designed electrolyte under equilibrium conditions, free from interference from external fields or concentration gradients.
· Li plating/stripping measurements in Li | |Li symmetric cells (Supplementary Fig. 24) demonstrate stable cycling in the Designed-Cl electrolyte for over 320 h.
· Comparison with an otherwise identical electrolyte formulation without LiNO3 highlights the critical role of LiNO3 in stabilizing the lithium negative electrode.
· In addition, linear sweep voltammetry (LSV, Supplementary Fig. 25) and chron- oamperometry (CA, Supplementary Fig. 26) measurements using Li | | Al and Li | |Ti cells verify that both the Designed-Cl and Designed-Br electrolytes maintain an oxidative stability window exceeding 3.7 V.
· Electrochemical performance of the Li | |Se cells The electrochemical performance of Li | |Se cells was evaluated using the Designed-Cl electrolyte.
· Notably, the peak positions remain essentially unchanged over the first three cycles, indicating good electrochemical reversibility in this electrolyte environment.
· The smaller hysteresis for Se0/Se+ couple relative to Se2⁻/Se0 further supports faster kinetics in the halide-mediated high- voltage conversion, consistent with the high diffusivity of Cl⁻ in the electrolyte.
· Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-77258-w Nature Communications Article in Press discharge capacity after 48 h of storage, indicating that the combina- tion of the KJ carbon host and tailored electrolyte can effectively anchor the charged products and suppress their diffusion.
· Constraining the operating window to 2.3 -3.3 V (Supplementary Fig. 32) isolates the Se0/Se+ chemistry and yields a redox couple at 2.66/2.57 V with a polarization of only ~90 mV, further evidencing the high effective Cl-activity in the electrolyte.
· The multi-electron conversion behavior is robust with respect to electrolyte composition: varying the TBMACl con- centration between 0.5 M and 2.0 M (Supplementary Fig. 36) or replacing TBMACl with TBMABr (Supplementary Figs. 37, 38) still preserves three-electron Se conversion.
· To demonstrate the versatility of the electrolyte-enabled high-valent chalcogen redox strategy, the Designed-Cl electrolyte was extended to Li | |S (Supplementary Figs. 40, 41) and Li | |SeS2 (Supplementary Figs. 42, 43) cells.
· Discussion In summary, we report an electrolyte-mediated route to unlock high- valent chalcogen redox chemistry in nonaqueous lithium batteries.
· By rationally designing halide-rich (Cl-or Br-) non-aqueous electrolytes, we enable a three-electron redox-amphoteric Se chemistry in Li | |Se cells, accessing a reversible Se2-/Se0/Se+ conversion that transcends the conventional two-electron Se0/Se2-limit.
· These metrics represent a 77% increase in capacity and a 64% increase in specific energy relative to the baseline electrolyte.
· The selection of halide salts with an asymmetric organic cation simulta- neously ensures high solubility and a high activity of halide anions.
· Furthermore, this electrolyte-enabled strategy can be extended to other chalcogen positive electrodes.
· When applied to Li | |S and Li | |SeS2 cells, the designed electrolyte activates analogous high-valent reactions of S and SeS2, leading to increased discharge capacity.
· Overall, this electrolyte strategy pro- vides a potential approach for accessing high-valent chalcogen redox chemistry and improving the energy density of lithium batteries.
· Preparation of electrolytes All the electrolytes were prepared in an Ar-filled glovebox ( < 1 ppm of water and O2).
· The baseline electrolyte consisted of 1 M LiTFSI dis- solved in a DME/DOL mixture (1:1, v/v), with 5 wt.% LiNO3 added as an additive.
· The designed halogen-rich electrolyte comprised 1 M LiTFSI, 1 M LiFSI, and 0.5 M LiNO3 dissolved in DMI, with the addition of either 1 M TBMACl or 1 M TBMABr.
· The solvent-to-solid mass ratio in the slurry was 3:1.
· The amount of electrolyte for each cell is 80 µL.
· Molecular dynamics (MD) simulation For the MD simulations, we have constructed the electrolyte models in a 43 × 43 × 43 Å box, which consists of 32 TBMACl, 16 LiNO3, 32 LiTFSI, and 32 LiFSI in the ionic condition with 296 DMI atoms.
· All ions and solvent molecules are randomly distributed in the simulated box.
· Before MD simulations, the initial electrolyte structures were pre-equilibrated via geometry optimiza- tions and annealing to release excessive strain.
· We adopted the NPT ensemble instead of NVT because the density of this highly con- centrated multi-salt electrolyte cannot be reliably predefined.

· ［SI］Supplementary Note 1 To evaluate the stability of Se2Cl2 under our electrolyte conditions, we examined the compatibility between Se2Cl2 and the Designed-Cl electrolyte.
· ［SI］After liquid Se2Cl2 was introduced into the Designed-Cl electrolyte, the reddish-brown Se2Cl2 phase remained at the bottom of the electrolyte because of the density difference, with only slight diffusion observed over time.
· ［SI］This result suggests that Se2Cl2 exhibits reasonable compatibility and stability in the Designed-Cl electrolyte environment.
· ［SI］Supplementary Note 2 To evaluate the adsorption ability of KJ toward Se2Cl2, 50 µL of Se2Cl2 was added into 8 mL of electrolyte and uniformly dispersed by shaking (Supplementary Fig. 4).
· ［SI］These results indicate that the synergistic effects of the electrolyte and positive electrode host help confine Se2Cl2 and suppress its diffusion during electrochemical cycling.
· ［SI］Supplementary Note 3 The stability of the Li metal negative electrode is critical for the overall performance of Li||Se cells, especially in halide-rich electrolytes.
· ［SI］SEM/EDS analysis of the region facing the Se positive electrode revealed the presence of N, O, F, and S species, which can be attributed to the SEI components derived from the decomposition of LiTFSI, LiFSI, and LiNO3.
· ［SI］These results suggest that the halide-rich electrolyte does not induce severe Se/Cl-containing deposition or passivation on the Li metal negative electrode under our testing conditions, thereby supporting the observed cycling stability of the Li||Se cells.
