#### P662 | 10.1038/s41560-026-02120-8 | Lithium-disulfur dichloride batteries
原判：Ⅰ=? 层=C 因素=DMA 基电解液（+LiDFOB）vs DOL-DME（S-LiCl 正极，锂–二氯化二硫） Ⅱ=N 备注=存疑：氯介导高价硫新化学，是否纳入待定
相关句 57（正文）+ 53（SI）
· Raising sulfur to higher oxidation states could increase cell voltage, yet reversible high-valence sulfur chemistry in lithium batteries remains difficult because chloride species bind Li+ and halogen-mediated reactions consume electrolyte.
· Here we show that a free-chloride-rich ionic liquid electrolyte enables a Li||S2Cl2 chemistry that addresses these limitations by reversibly converting Li2S to S2Cl2 through a three-electron sulfur redox process.
· The electrolyte functions as an ionic mediator with only a minor capacity contribution.
· Advances in conductive and catalytic hosts, electrolytes and separators have improved sulfur utilization and mitigated polysulfide shuttling3-6.
· Nevertheless, major challenges remain, particularly the low redox potential of the S2-/S0 conversion and the severe electrolyte requirements associated with practical high-areal-capacity operation7,8.
· This demand requires electrolytes that sustain high critical current densities while suppressing lithium dendrites and polysulfide shuttling8, beyond current Li-S electrolytes10-13.
· In addition, highly coor- dinated electrolytes that form stable LiF-rich interphases often restrict ion mobility and increase viscosity14,15.
· Thus, no existing electrolyte simul- taneously satisfies high ionic conductivity, high critical current density and low polysulfide solubility for practical low-voltage Li-S batteries.
· Exploring high-valence sulfur redox chemistry provides a prom- ising strategy to address these limitations by increasing the sulfur redox potential beyond the conventional S2-/S0 couple, thereby simultaneously improving operating voltage and energy density while alleviating the stringent electrolyte requirements associ…
· In addition, most reported Cl--assisted sulfur oxidation chemistries operate through dual-ion mechanisms in which the electrolyte directly participates in charge storage, thereby lowering the practical energy density and causing sustained electrolyte consumption25.
· Developing a lithium-compatible chlorine-mediated sulfur chemistry that enables reversible sulfur oxidation without con- tinuous electrolyte depletion therefore remains a critical challenge for high-energy-density Li-S batteries.
· A free-Cl--rich ionic liquid electro- lyte enables sulfur oxidation to S1+ at ~3.4 V through dynamic chloride exchange between the electrolyte and the electrode, resulting in an overall reversible three-electron sulfur redox process.
· The electrochemical performance of Li||S-LiCl cells was first evaluated using a conventional Li-S electrolyte consisting of 1 M lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LiTFSI) dissolved in a 1:1 (v/v) mixture of 1,3-dioxolane (DOL) and 1,2-dimethoxyethane (DME).
· As shown in Fig. 2a, the voltage profiles of the S-LiCl electrode were nearly identical to those of the S-C electrode, indicating that little or no S0/S1+ conversion occurred in the DOL-DME electrolyte.
· In addition, the low solubility of LiCl in the DOL-DME electrolyte limited interactions between sulfur and LiCl primarily to solid-solid interfaces.
· An effective strategy to improve the reaction kinetics is to shift from a solid-solid to a solid-liquid reaction by designing electrolytes capable of partially dissolving LiCl.
· Among the strongly solvating solvents evaluated, N,N-dimethylacetamide (DMA) exhibited the high- est LiCl solubility (>2 M; Supplementary Fig. 3).
· Consequently, 0.9 M LiTFSI in DMA was selected as the base electrolyte, with 0.1 M lithium difluoro(oxalato)borate (LiDFOB) added to suppress polysulfide shut- tling28.
· Linear sweep voltammetry (LSV; Supplementary Fig. 4) showed that the electrolyte remained stable up to ~4.5 V, confirming its suitabil- ity for Li||S2Cl2 chemistry.
· As shown in Fig. 2b and Supplementary Fig. 5, the first discharge profile of the S-LiCl electrode in the DMA electrolyte exhibited a polysulfide plateau at ~2.6 V, slightly higher than the ~2.3 V plateau observed in DOL-DME electrolytes.
· The discharge capacity was comparable to that obtained in DOL-DME electrolytes.
· To further enhance Cl- activity, 1-ethyl-3-methylimidazolium chlo- ride (EMIMCl, ~1 M solubility in DMA) was introduced to increase the Cl- concentration, while the lithium salt concentration was reduced to maintain a total solute concentration of 1.0 M.
· The LSV results con- firmed that the DMA electrolyte containing 1 M EMIMCl remained electrochemically stable up to ~4.0 V (Supplementary Fig. 4).
· Figure 2c shows the charge profiles of fully lithiated electrodes (Li2S + LiCl) in DMA electrolytes containing (1 - x) M LiTFSI and x M EMIMCl at different x values.
· To gain insight into the electrolyte design, molecular dynamics (MD) simulations were performed on the (1 - x) M LiTFSI + x M EMIMCl DMA electrolytes to examine the local solvation structure of Cl-.
· The extended charge plateau in the x = 0.9 electrolyte, which exceeds the theoretical capacity, further supports this interpretation.
· To suppress this shuttle effect, the highly solvating DMA electrolyte needs to be replaced with a phase-separating electrolyte designed to immobilize S2Cl2 through liquid-liquid immiscibility and thereby prevent its diffusion to the lithium metal.
· The LiTFSI- EMIMCl ionic liquid can therefore be used as the electrolyte system either directly or diluted with fluorinated ethers such as 1,1,2,2-tetrafluoroethyl 2,2,3,3-tetrafluoropropyl ether (TTE) to reduce viscosity and suppress S2Cl2 dissolution.
· Two electrolytes were subse- quently prepared: a diluted ionic liquid of LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE (1:9:10; Supplementary Fig. 12) and a pure ionic liquid of LiTFSI-EMIMCl (1:9).
· As shown in Supplementary Figs. 13-15, the diluted electrolyte exhib- ited higher ionic conductivity (~3.5 mS cm-1), mainly because of its lower viscosity, and a higher Li+ transference number (tLi+) ≈ 0.35 than the pure ionic liquid.
· S2Cl2 also remains immiscible in the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte (Supplementary Fig. 16b).
· In this electrolyte, TTE functions primarily as a diluent to reduce the viscosity of the ionic liquid, while the electrolyte simultaneously satisfies several key requirements: dissolving Cl- species, oversaturating the Li+ coordi- nation environment with Cl-, suppressing high-order polysulfide disso- lution and phase-s…
· MD simulations of the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE (1:9:10) electrolyte further showed that ~67% of Cl- ions remain uncoordinated and available to participate in sulfur oxi- dation (Supplementary Fig. 17).
· Because electrolytes containing Cl- can corrode aluminium current collectors at elevated voltages32, Li||Al cells were evaluated using the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte.
· In addition, Li||carbon cells only exhibited a small reversible capacity of ~20 mAh gC -1 (Supplementary Fig. 18b), indicating negligible electro- chemical activity of the electrolyte between 1.6 V and 3.6 V.
· As illustrated in Fig. 3a, the S2Cl2 generated during charging is initially retained within the mesoporous carbon matrix but readily dis- solves in DMA-based electrolytes because of its high solubility.
· By contrast, in the LiTFSI- EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte (Fig. 3b), S2Cl2 is largely retained within the mesoporous carbon electrode, probably because of its immiscibility Nature Energy | Volume 11 | September 2026 | 1297-1306 1300 Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41560-026-02120-8 and phase separation from the electrolyte.
· The electrolyte also sup- presses polysulfide shuttling, enabling reversible sulfur redox from S2- to S1+.
· For comparison, the conventional two-electron sulfur redox process in 1 M LiTFSI in DOL- DME electrolyte delivers a reversible capacity of ~1,165 mAh gS -1 with an average discharge voltage of ~2.05 V at 25 °C and 0.2C (Fig. 3c).
· Cell performance of Li||S-LiCl batteries The redox mechanism of the S-LiCl electrode in the LiTFSI-EMIMCl- TTE electrolyte has been established.
· To improve cycling per- formance, 60% of LiTFSI was replaced by a combination of lithium bis(fluorosulfonyl)imide (LiFSI) and lithium nitrate (LiNO3) while maintaining a Li/Cl ratio of 1:9.
· The resulting electrolyte, denoted LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE-A, has a LiTFSI:LiFSI:LiNO3 ratio of 4:4:2 (Sup- plementary Note 2 and Supplementary Figs. 29 and 30).
· The Li||S-LiCl cell with the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE-A electrolyte was first subjected to five formation cycles at a charging rate of 0.2C and a discharging rate of 0.1C (1C = 700 mAh g-1) within a voltage window of 1.8-3.55 V, followed by cycling at 0.3C for charging and 0.2C for discharging.
· Insets show S2Cl2 dissolved in DMA and immiscible in TTE. c, Voltage profiles comparing the three- electron Li2S/S2Cl2 redox process in the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte with the conventional two-electron sulfur redox process in 1 M LiTFSI in DOL-DME electrolyte. d, CV curves comparing the three-electron Li2S/S2Cl2 red…
· Nature Energy | Volume 11 | September 2026 | 1297-1306 1302 Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41560-026-02120-8 100 cycles, the S-LiCl electrode retained ~70% of its initial capac- ity in the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE-A electrolyte (Fig. 5a, orange dots).
· The LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE-A electrolyte also improved the cycling stability of conventional S-C electrodes compared with 1 M LiTFSI in DOL- DME (Supplementary Fig. 33), consistent with the suppressed poly- sulfide solubility shown in Supplementary Fig. 34.
· Before this comparison, we evaluated the electrolyte contribution and found that the energy density arises mainly from the S-LiCl electrode materi- als, with only a minor contribution from the electrolyte, consistent with conventional lithium-ion batteries (Supplementary Note 3 and Supplementary Figs. 35-39).
· Stack-level specific energy and energy density were further estimated by accounting for the masses of the electrodes, electrolyte, separator and current collectors.
· Nature Energy | Volume 11 | September 2026 | 1297-1306 1303 Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41560-026-02120-8 operating voltage of the Li||S2Cl2 chemistry also reduces the electrolyte requirements needed to achieve a given energy output compared with conventional sulfur and sulfurized polyacrylonitrile (SPAN) electrod…
· Enabled by a free-Cl--rich ionic liquid electrolyte, the S-LiCl electrode delivered a reversible capacity of 713 mAh g-1, corresponding to 1,838 mAh gS -1, at an average operating voltage of 2.54 V.
· This high-voltage sulfur chemis- try achieves an electrode-level specific energy exceeding 1,700 Wh kg-1 with only a minor energy contribution from the electrolyte.
· Methods Materials and electrolytes All chemicals were used as received unless otherwise specified.
· DMA (99.8%), DOL (≥99.5%), DME (99.5%), triethylene glycol dimethyl ether (99%), dimethyl sulfoxide (99.9%), 1,3-dimethyl-2-imidazolidinone (99.0%), tetramethylurea (99.0%), N-methyl-2-pyrrolidone (NMP, 99.5%), disulfur dichloride (S2Cl2, 98%), sulfur powder (S8, 99.98% trace metals basis), lithium chlo- ride (LiCl, 99…
· Electrolytes were prepared in an argon-filled glove- box (O2 ≤ 0.1 ppm, H2O ≤ 0.1 ppm) by dissolving the required salts in the corresponding solvents according to the formulations described in the main text.
· Unless otherwise specified, each coin cell con- tained 20 µl of electrolyte, corresponding to an electrolyte-to-sulfur ratio of ~50-60 ml g-1 sulfur.
· Pouch cells were assembled using a similar configuration, except that only one separator layer was used and the electrolyte-to-sulfur ratio was reduced to 10 ml g-1 sulfur.
· For Li||Li symmetric cells, a single cellulose separator and ~20 µl electrolyte were used per cell.
· ［SI］Supplementary Note 2: Optimization of the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte Although the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte (LiTFSI, lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide; EMIMCl, 1-ethyl-3-methylimidazolium chloride; TTE, 1,1,2,2-tetrafluoroethyl 2,2,3,3-tetrafluoropropyl ether) exhibits good reversibility for Li||S-L…
· ［SI］As shown in Supplementary Figure 29b, Li symmetric cells using the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte remain stable for only ~10 cycles at a current density of 0.2 mA cm-2 and an areal capacity of 0.4 mAh cm-2.
· ［SI］Both lithium bis(fluorosulfonyl)imide (LiFSI) and lithium nitrate (LiNO3) are widely used additives for improving the cycling stability of lithium metal.3,4 After partially replacing 60% of LiTFSI with 40% LiFSI and 20% LiNO3, the lithium cycling stability improved significantly.
· ［SI］This modified electrolyte is denoted as LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE-A.
· ［SI］The lithium surface cycled in LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE exhibited stronger C-O, C=O, and - CO3-/CFx signals in the C 1s spectra, indicating a larger fraction of organic species in the solid electrolyte interphase (SEI).
· ［SI］The improved lithium reversibility in the modified electrolyte is therefore attributed to the formation of this robust LiF-rich interphase.
· ［SI］Supplementary Note 3: Evaluation of energy contribution from the electrolyte The presence of Cl- in the electrolyte may raise concerns regarding its possible contribution to the overall energy density of the cell and the practical evaluation of Li||S- LiCl cells.
· ［SI］To address this issue, two key points must be clarified: (1) the operation of the Li||S2Cl2 chemistry does not rely on energy stored in the electrolyte itself, and (2) the contribution of the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte to the total cell energy is minimal.
· ［SI］As discussed in the main text, the introduction of EMIMCl into the electrolyte is intended to introduce excess Cl- relative to the available Li+ coordination sites and thereby increase the fraction of free Cl- species.
· ［SI］Importantly, sulfur oxidation can still occur even in the absence of Cl- in the electrolyte, provided that LiCl is incorporated within the electrode.
· ［SI］To verify this, triethylene glycol dimethyl ether (G3) was selected as the solvent because of its strong coordination with Li+, which is expected to weaken Li+-Cl- interactions.
· ［SI］As shown in Supplementary Figure 35a, Li||S-LiCl cells using a G3-based electrolyte (2 M lithium difluoro(oxalato)borate (LiDFOB) + 1 M LiNO3) exhibit sulfur oxidation at ~3.7-3.8 V, approximately 0.3 V above the theoretical sulfur oxidation voltage but still below the oxidation potential of pure LiCl (~3.9-4.0 V).
· ［SI］A small cathodic peak near ~3.2-3.3 V is observed in the magnified dQ/dV profile (inset of Supplementary Figure 35b), indicating that only a small fraction of S2Cl2 remains within the electrode because most S2Cl2 dissolves into the electrolyte and undergoes severe shuttle behavior, leading to rapid capacity decay.
· ［SI］Sulfur oxidation therefore primarily originates from the S-LiCl electrode rather than from electrochemical energy stored in the electrolyte.
· ［SI］At the same time, the strong Li+-Cl- interactions in the G3 electrolyte further highlight the importance of generating free Cl- species to facilitate reversible sulfur oxidation.
· ［SI］To further evaluate the possible energy contribution from the electrolyte, Li||S-C cells with areal capacities of ~0.6-0.7 mAh cm-2 were tested using either pristine LiTFSI- EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte or LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte saturated with LiCl.
· ［SI］As shown in Supplementary Figure 36, the conventional S2-/S0 redox reaction proceeds normally in both electrolytes, whereas the high-voltage sulfur oxidation process contributes only ~30 mAh gS-1 in the LiCl-free electrolyte and ~50 mAh gS-1 in the LiCl- saturated electrolyte.
· ［SI］These capacities are negligible compared with the total capacity of the S-LiCl electrode, indicating that Cl- species originating only from the electrolyte are insufficient to sustain substantial sulfur oxidation.
· ［SI］In this mechanism, free Cl- species in the electrolyte initiate sulfur oxidation, while LiCl within the electrode continuously dissolves to replenish the Cl- consumed during cycling.
· ［SI］As a result, the overall Cl- concentration in the electrolyte remains approximately constant during operation.
· ［SI］To support this mechanism, Scanning electron microscopy (SEM)/energy-dispersive X- ray spectroscopy (EDS) analyses were performed on S-LiCl electrodes recovered at different voltages (2.6, 3.2, and 3.6 V) in the LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte (Supplementary Figures 37-39).
· ［SI］The persistence of undissolved LiCl at intermediate voltages further confirms that the electrolyte alone does not fully dissolve LiCl within the electrode.
· ［SI］The LiTFSI-EMIMCl-TTE electrolyte therefore functions primarily as a chlorine mediator rather than a major energy-storage component.
· ［SI］Based on the measured electrochemical response of S-C electrodes, the direct energy contribution from the electrolyte is estimated to be ~2-3% of the total cell energy.

· EES Batteries PAPER Cite this: EES Batteries, 2026, 2, 1247 Received 3rd March 2026, Accepted 6th May 2026 DOI: 10.1039/d6eb00050a rsc.li/EESBatteries Secondary alkali-metal salts in supporting electrolytes for lithium polysulfide flow batteries Thomas J.
· Alkali-metal additives have previously been shown to help regulate anode plating in lithium-metal batteries, and in the present work we investigate the eﬀects of secondary alkali- metal bis(trifluoromethylsulfonyl)imide (MTFSI; M = Na, K, Rb, Cs) additives on the electrochemical behav- iour of LiPS electrolytes under s…
· We have synthesised a full series of MTFSI salts and report the first solvent-free crystal structure of RbTFSI, completing the family of unsolvated alkali- metal TFSI complexes.
· Electrochemical characterisation (CV, GCD, EIS) reveals that NaTFSI and RbTFSI improve key performance metrics, including electrolyte conductivity, capacity retention, and coulombic eﬃciency, despite evidence of side reactions with polysulfides.
· This work addresses that challenge by investigating how secondary alkali-metal salts, specifically NaTFSI, KTFSI, RbTFSI, and CsTFSI, interact within LiPS electrolytes and influence battery behaviour.
· We focus on whether these additives can stabilise the anode or otherwise mitigate degradation processes.
· Our results show that sodium and rubidium salts can improve energy eﬃciency and cycling stability, even though they also participate in side reactions that consume some active material.
· The discovery of the first solvent-free crystal structure of RbTFSI further broadens understanding of electrolyte design and ion-solvent interactions.
· By clarifying how secondary cations influence both beneficial and detrimental processes in LiPS systems, this study provides a foundation for more robust electrolyte formulations that could accelerate the deployment of aﬀordable, sustainable, long-duration energy storage.
· In eﬀorts to avoid the formation of elemental sulfur and Li2S, both of which are insoluble in ether-based electrolytes and highly resistive, the potential window of operation is smaller than traditional LSBs, resulting in a lower theoretical specific capacity of the cell (209 mAh g-1), and hence the LiPS flow battery i…
· In this work we appraise the LiPS battery chemistry in a coin cell con- figuration which allows for cheaper and rapid prototyping of electrolyte formulations.
· Synthesis of LiPS catholyte Catholyte was synthesized from elemental lithium (Sigma- Aldrich, 99% trace metal basis) and sulfur (Sigma-Aldrich, 99.5%) at a molar ratio of 2 : 6 with sulfur concentration at 2 M.
· After the solution had cooled, varying concentrations of LiTFSI (Fisher Scientific, 99%, anhydrous) and MTFSI salts alongside 0.3 M LiNO3 (Fisher Scientific, 99%, anhydrous) were added to the catholyte, and left to dissolve for 3 h.
· Although CsTFSI salt was prepared, it did not dissolve into the DOL/DME electrolyte solution; for this reason, electro- chemical testing of this additive was not possible.
· Catholyte was pipetted (100 µL) onto the carbon felt.
· All cell components excluding Li metal and the catholyte were dried in a vacuum oven at 100 °C for 5 h to remove any adhered or absorbed water, and assembled in an Ar-filled glovebox.
· The electrolyte used for the CV cells had a lower LiPS concentration of 20 mM, to increase the stability of the response obtained in the CV, while the concentration of all other species remained identical to other electrolytes tested.
· Results and discussion Crystallography We attempted to recrystallize the alkali-metal TFSI salts from a concentrated DOL/DME solution to determine if there were any interactions with the Lewis basic solvents.

· LiNO3 has been added to the cath- olyte used in the CVs to determine if any side reactions occur between the MTFSI salts and LiNO3, throughout the entire voltage window of operation.
· The addition of LiNO3, or other salts, is not common practice in literature when performing CVs, since the primary goal is often to monitor how the sulfur kinetics change, but by adding LiNO3 we are able to see any potential interactions between the salts that would be present during normal operation of the battery.

· We have found these cells to be a useful tool for determining salt compatibility and allow screening of formulations without having to produce the larger quantities required for flow cell operation.
· In the GCD curves it can be seen that the addition of any MTFSI salt at any concentration decreases the capacity of the cell compared to the standard cell at low discharge rates (Fig. 3b-d), with the exception of Na+ at 0.1 M.

· Despite the suspected side reactions between the alkali-metal ions and sulfur for both the KTFSI and RbTFSI additives, the only obvious change in the GCD voltage profile (Fig. 3e) is an increased initial irreversible loss for the Na and Rb salts.

· Conclusions In this research we have synthesised alkali-metal TFSI salts for use as a co-additive in LiPS batteries alongside more tra- ditional additives.
· These co-additives oﬀer improvements in key performance parameters, such as capacity, capacity reten- tion and coulombic eﬃciency, at high concentrations (0.1 M) of the sodium and rubidium salts.
· Additionally, through single crystal XRD we report the first example of solvent-free RbTFSI, continuing and completing the family of unsolvated alkali-metal TFSI complexes.
· It was initially hypothesised that cells would benefit from MTFSI additives due to the SHES mechanism, previously reported by Ding et al., but clear evi- dence is provided of side reactions between the MTFSI salts and the LiPS active material, established through electro- chemical methods and surface analysis of the el…
· Additionally, the additives should be tested in a flow battery configuration to confirm the findings of the static cell.
· Alongside being one of the better performing salts, NaTFSI is currently commercially available, due to its use in sodium-ion chemistries, which may make it a more appealing choice over more bespoke additives for industry.
· ［SI］EES Batteries PAPER Cite this: EES Batteries, 2026, 2, 1247 Received 3rd March 2026, Accepted 6th May 2026 DOI: 10.1039/d6eb00050a rsc.li/EESBatteries Secondary alkali-metal salts in supporting electrolytes for lithium polysulfide flow batteries Thomas J.
· ［SI］Alkali-metal additives have previously been shown to help regulate anode plating in lithium-metal batteries, and in the present work we investigate the eﬀects of secondary alkali- metal bis(trifluoromethylsulfonyl)imide (MTFSI; M = Na, K, Rb, Cs) additives on the electrochemical behav- iour of LiPS electrolytes und…
· ［SI］We have synthesised a full series of MTFSI salts and report the first solvent-free crystal structure of RbTFSI, completing the family of unsolvated alkali- metal TFSI complexes.
· ［SI］Electrochemical characterisation (CV, GCD, EIS) reveals that NaTFSI and RbTFSI improve key performance metrics, including electrolyte conductivity, capacity retention, and coulombic eﬃciency, despite evidence of side reactions with polysulfides.
· ［SI］This work addresses that challenge by investigating how secondary alkali-metal salts, specifically NaTFSI, KTFSI, RbTFSI, and CsTFSI, interact within LiPS electrolytes and influence battery behaviour.
· ［SI］We focus on whether these additives can stabilise the anode or otherwise mitigate degradation processes.
· ［SI］Our results show that sodium and rubidium salts can improve energy eﬃciency and cycling stability, even though they also participate in side reactions that consume some active material.
· ［SI］The discovery of the first solvent-free crystal structure of RbTFSI further broadens understanding of electrolyte design and ion-solvent interactions.
· ［SI］By clarifying how secondary cations influence both beneficial and detrimental processes in LiPS systems, this study provides a foundation for more robust electrolyte formulations that could accelerate the deployment of aﬀordable, sustainable, long-duration energy storage.
· ［SI］In eﬀorts to avoid the formation of elemental sulfur and Li2S, both of which are insoluble in ether-based electrolytes and highly resistive, the potential window of operation is smaller than traditional LSBs, resulting in a lower theoretical specific capacity of the cell (209 mAh g-1), and hence the LiPS flow batte…
· ［SI］In this work we appraise the LiPS battery chemistry in a coin cell con- figuration which allows for cheaper and rapid prototyping of electrolyte formulations.
· ［SI］Synthesis of LiPS catholyte Catholyte was synthesized from elemental lithium (Sigma- Aldrich, 99% trace metal basis) and sulfur (Sigma-Aldrich, 99.5%) at a molar ratio of 2 : 6 with sulfur concentration at 2 M.
· ［SI］After the solution had cooled, varying concentrations of LiTFSI (Fisher Scientific, 99%, anhydrous) and MTFSI salts alongside 0.3 M LiNO3 (Fisher Scientific, 99%, anhydrous) were added to the catholyte, and left to dissolve for 3 h.
· ［SI］Although CsTFSI salt was prepared, it did not dissolve into the DOL/DME electrolyte solution; for this reason, electro- chemical testing of this additive was not possible.
· ［SI］Catholyte was pipetted (100 µL) onto the carbon felt.
· ［SI］All cell components excluding Li metal and the catholyte were dried in a vacuum oven at 100 °C for 5 h to remove any adhered or absorbed water, and assembled in an Ar-filled glovebox.
· ［SI］The electrolyte used for the CV cells had a lower LiPS concentration of 20 mM, to increase the stability of the response obtained in the CV, while the concentration of all other species remained identical to other electrolytes tested.
· ［SI］Results and discussion Crystallography We attempted to recrystallize the alkali-metal TFSI salts from a concentrated DOL/DME solution to determine if there were any interactions with the Lewis basic solvents.

· ［SI］Galvanostatic charge-discharge testing For each of the salts tested, a comparison to a standard static cell with 1 M LiTFSI and 0.3 M LiNO3 was made.
· ［SI］The catho- lytes tested with MTFSI salts had a total TFSI-anion concen- tration of 1 M for all cells, alongside 0.3 M LiNO3 (e.g. a cell with 0.1 M MTFSI only had 0.9 M LiTFSI).
· ［SI］We have found these cells to be a useful tool for determining salt compatibility and allow screening of formulations without having to produce the larger quantities required for flow cell operation.
· ［SI］In the GCD curves it can be seen that the addition of any MTFSI salt at any concentration decreases the capacity of the cell compared to the standard cell at low discharge rates (Fig. 3b-d), with the exception of Na+ at 0.1 M.
· ［SI］The decreased capacity obtained with most of these salts, whilst undesirable, is not necessarily a drawback as this work is prototyping for- mulations for a flow battery configuration which allows power and capacity to be scaled independently, so if other perform- ance characteristics can be shown to improve with M…

· ［SI］According to the proposed SHES mechanism, RbTFSI was expected to oﬀer the best cell performance of the additives tested due to the increased ionic radii of Rb ions compared to the other alkali-metals tested herein.

· ［SI］The theoretical capacity of the cells is 80 Ah L-1, based on the quantity of sulfur active material dissolved in the catholyte.
· ［SI］As the volume of catholyte was kept constant for each of the cells, the mass of the catholyte used in the cell increases slightly as the alkali-metal present in the salt increases in atomic number.
· ［SI］Despite the increased mass this has a small impact on the total mass of solution with a percentage increase compared to the standard of 0.13%, 0.26% and 0.62% for the Na, K, and Rb salts respectively for the highest concen- trations of MTFSI salts used.
· ［SI］The EIS spectra for the standard cell (Fig. 4a), containing none of the synthesized MTFSI salts, shows increasing resis- tance from all sources (electrolyte, anode & cathode).
· ［SI］This indi- cates that the additives are continuously being consumed to form an SEI layer on the anode and the loss of conductive ions from solution increases electrolyte resistance.

· ［SI］The behaviour of the cathodic charge transfer resistance, caused by the charge transfer of LiPS, is altered by the pres- ence of MTFSI salt (Fig. 4f).

· ［SI］Again, the changes observed for the cathode in a KTFSI cell diﬀers to that seen with the other MTFSI salts: a sudden spike in resistance is observed with KTFSI followed by a gradual relaxation of cell re- sistance.
· ［SI］The higher frequency semi-circle in each of the Nyquist plots has been attributed to the transport of Li+ through the SEI layer on the Li anode.33 For each MTFSI salt the resistance from the Li+ passing through the SEI is increased compared to LiTFSI only (Fig. 4g).

· ［SI］By preventing the formation of high concentrations of short chain insoluble LiPS it is possible that LiNO3 is being consumed via a diﬀerent mechanism.
· ［SI］The apparent change in the behaviour of LiNO3 within the cell cannot be directly attributed to the presence of MTFSI since the anode from the standard cell also lacks any NOx species.

· ［SI］Conclusions In this research we have synthesised alkali-metal TFSI salts for use as a co-additive in LiPS batteries alongside more tra- ditional additives.
· ［SI］These co-additives oﬀer improvements in key performance parameters, such as capacity, capacity reten- tion and coulombic eﬃciency, at high concentrations (0.1 M) of the sodium and rubidium salts.
· ［SI］Additionally, through single crystal XRD we report the first example of solvent-free RbTFSI, continuing and completing the family of unsolvated alkali-metal TFSI complexes.
· ［SI］It was initially hypothesised that cells would benefit from MTFSI additives due to the SHES mechanism, previously reported by Ding et al., but clear evi- dence is provided of side reactions between the MTFSI salts and the LiPS active material, established through electro- chemical methods and surface analysis of th…
· ［SI］Additionally, the additives should be tested in a flow battery configuration to confirm the findings of the static cell.
· ［SI］Alongside being one of the better performing salts, NaTFSI is currently commercially available, due to its use in sodium-ion chemistries, which may make it a more appealing choice over more bespoke additives for industry.
